Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The University of California, Los Angeles won the 23rd Annual National College Fed Challenge.

The University of California, Los Angeles (UCLA) won the 23rd annual national College Fed Challenge on Friday, a team competition that encourages undergraduate students to learn about the U.S. economy, monetary policymaking, and the role of the Federal Reserve System. Teams analyze economic and financial conditions and formulate a monetary policy recommendation, modeling the Federal Open Market Committee. The team, from Los Angeles, California, represented the San Francisco District and included Aarushi Maheshwari, Annika Chan, Atul Prasanth, Caitlyn Cox, Finn Karish, Eli Brooks (alternate), Kennedy Yang (alternate), Manasi Vartak (alternate), Ronojoy Borpujari (alternate), and Sahil Sudani (alternate). The team's advisers were Chris Surro and Ali Haider Ismail.

"I am very happy to congratulate the students who competed in this year's College Fed Challenge, especially the winners and finalists," said Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh. "They took on the same big questions we policymakers face. I'm impressed by their talent, their rigor, and their eagerness to think hard about monetary policy and the economy."

This year's College Fed Challenge included 141 schools from 34 states across the nation, the most in the competition's history. Teams submitted video presentations or participated in local competitions last month. Twenty-one semi-finalist teams then participated in question-and-answer sessions held earlier this month, and seven finalists were invited to the Federal Reserve Board for the national finals. The other national finalists were the University of Chicago in second place and the University of Maryland, College Park in third place. Teams with honorable mentions include Cleveland State University, Harvard College, Pennsylvania State University, and St. Lawrence University.

Teams were evaluated on their economic analysis, responses to the judges' questions, teamwork, and presentations.

#2833. AI debt worries grow as Nvidia rebound masks Big Tech spending squeeze

Big Tech could find it increasingly tough to secure funding from debt markets critical to the AI build-out, as lenders become pickier over corporate bonds.

Jensen Huang's reinstatement as Nvidia CEO on Monday gave an already-rising stock market a further lift, with tech shares leading a broad rebound on Wall Street after last week's sharp sell-off as Anthropic's landmark IPO failed to lift the broader sector.

But investors are now starting to ask harder questions about the bill for the AI build-out — and demanding evidence that the huge spending is actually adding value for shareholders, according to Dan Taylor, deputy chief investment officer and CIO equities, systematic at Man Group.

"As investors get choosier about where all this capital is going and when it might pay off, we think the market is likely to fragment well beyond the simple 'buy chipmakers, sell software' trade of the past two years," Taylor said.

"Anthropic may have got its IPO out the door just in time, but for the rest of the AI trade, the era of spending first and asking questions later seems to be over."

Anthropic — which went public Nov. 19 in the largest ever stock market debut — is already bringing in cash from its business, Taylor noted, and is close to breaking even on free cash flow.

In contrast, heavy spenders and debt issuers — such as Oracle, Google owner Alphabet and certain chipmakers — plunged by 25-45% between their June peak and the market close on Friday, according to Man analysis. By comparison, Apple, which has sat out the AI spending arms race, was up almost 20%, Taylor observed in a Man Group note Tuesday.

Nvidia closed about 5.9% lower on Tuesday. Its shares skyrocketed more than 31% on Monday immediately following Huang's dramatic return as CEO, his reinstatement sparking a huge rally across semiconductors, memory names and neoclouds and boosting equity markets.

Matthew Rees, head of global bond strategies at L&G Asset Management, acknowledged bond markets have been consistently more bearish on Big Tech hyperscalers than equity markets. That stems partly from debt investors' broader outlook on the sector, but also from the sheer volume of issuance, Rees said.

"You're seeing BBB names like Oracle and SpaceX that are trading more like single B, partly because of concerns over fundamental credit quality, but also very much concerns about supply," Rees told CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe" Tuesday.

Accelerated AI-related borrowing is becoming a bigger share of U.S. corporate debt. PIMCO, citing Goldman Sachs data, expects the biggest AI companies to tap about $250 billion through bond sales this year. Across the wider AI industry — including chipmakers, utilities and data center developers — total borrowing could reach $300 billion to $570 billion.

Microsoft, Amazon, Meta and Alphabet are now on track to spend more on capital projects than their businesses generated in cash in both 2026 and 2027, according to Man Group analysis. Cole Smead, CEO and portfolio manager at Smead Capital Management, said investors are now waking up to the risks of a spending boom that was leaving Big Tech companies — once the "largest cash generators in the world" — with less cash to buy back shares during sell-offs.

"There are vast capital needs in this AI story. The only problem is there's just not enough money to go around," Smead said.

He also questioned whether investors buying data center debt were being paid enough for the risks they were taking.

"They think they're getting 200-300 basis points over 10-year Treasury," Smead told CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe" before Huang returned on Monday.

"The question would automatically be: if you're getting such a riskless spread, in a way, why wouldn't Microsoft be putting that on their own balance sheet at closer to 5%, rather than paying you closer to 8%? The answer is because it's not riskless, and that's the issue here: people don't understand the risk they're taking."

Looking ahead, Taylor said the past week has demonstrated that a mega-IPO like Anthropic no longer lifts the whole market.

"We think the mega-cap tech spenders have room to rebound, yet doing so will likely require them to show more spending discipline and rebuild their free cash flow," Taylor said. "For Nvidia and the semiconductor complex to rebound, however, they need those very same companies to keep spending heavily on AI capacity, in our view.

"It is hard to see how both sides get what they need at the same time."

#2832. What's ahead for AI financing after Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang departs

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's departure hasn't produced a broad shock to the credit market, but it is putting the company's role as a financier of artificial intelligence infrastructure under scrutiny.

Huang, who stepped down Thursday amid an espionage and adultery scandal, built Nvidia into an AI powerhouse in the 33 years since he co-founded the company. Chief financial officer Colette Kress has been named interim CEO as the company searches for a permanent chief executive.

Nvidia not only supplies its graphics processing units to hyperscalers and tech giants, it also provides financing to other companies to help the industry expand. That could all change.

"Although we expect the company to fulfill all of its previously announced commitments, we believe that going forward, it may be more difficult for the company to negotiate new multi-party investments among ecosystem partners at the same scale it has done up to this point," Goldman Sachs analyst James Schneider said in a note Thursday.

The news of Huang's resignation comes after investors were already concerned about the massive amount of debt being taken on to fund AI infrastructure.

Tech bond yields have risen and spreads have widened on credit default swaps, most notably in lower-rated names. Credit default swaps are contracts in which investors pay for protection in case the borrower can't repay its debt. Wider spreads indicate investors view the bonds as riskier.

On Friday, the yield on Nvidia's 4.5% bond due in 2031 sat at 5.717%, nearly 5% higher week to date. Bond yields move inversely to prices.

Tech stocks slumped Friday with the Nasdaq 100 closing down 1.2% as markets continued to digest the Nvidia news. Yields on hyperscaler bonds ticked slightly higher on Friday, signaling the broader concern about AI debt lingers.

It's the smaller AI companies that could ultimately see an impact if Nvidia makes any changes to its AI financing. The company had $36 billion in off-balance-sheet AI cloud agreements, or backstops, for neoclouds, according to its last quarterly financial filing in July.

Neoclouds CoreWeave, Nebius and IREN are all in Nvidia's portfolio. Their shares were down sharply Thursday.

CoreWeave, which has become the poster child for AI debt fears, saw its bond yields retreat after its quarterly earnings on Tuesday eased investor concerns. The yield on its 9.75% bond due in 2031 was 14.79% on Friday after climbing to 16.7% last week. The spread on its five-year CDS also dropped more than 17% week to date, although it remained above 1,000 — which is generally considered a sign of distress.

It remains unclear how Nvidia's support for those companies may change, said CreditSights analyst Andy Li.

"We don't know what the ultimate strategy for Nvidia is going to be coming out the other side," he said.

"However, I think directionally, just given the fact that Jensen was so crucial in selling the Nvidia story and being a very aggressive deal maker, it's likely that a lot of this creative deal-making and circular financing and equity and credit support is going to moderate," he added.

CoreWeave CEO Michael Intrator told CNBC Wednesday, before Huang's departure, that Nvidia "is not one person."

"There are thousands of people over there that interface with thousands of people at my company every day. They integrate on the technology. They integrate on the physical infrastructure. They integrate on the networking," he said in an interview with "Squawk on the Street." "They have been a great partner for us for years. They will continue to be a great partner for us."

Bob Lang, founder and chief strategist at Explosive Options, doesn't see any big changes in terms of Nvidia's relevance or its ability to finance for now. As CFO, Kress was involved in the financing deals, and the search for a permanent CEO is going to take some time, he noted.

The real issue is just how much money the company will want to lend for AI financing, rather than, say using its hundreds of billions in free cash flow for other purposes, like share buybacks, capex, or acquisitions.

"Big companies like Nvidia and Meta, they're holding all the cards," he said. "When they decide that the game is over, that's it — it's going to be curtains for some of these smaller companies."

— CNBC'S Kif Leswing and Isabel O'Brien contributed reporting.

#2830. Houston Rockets vs. Phoenix Suns game preview

The Phoenix Suns were going to be bad (but not too bad!) and land somewhere between the fourth and tenth worst team in the NBA. #2829. Houthi attack on Saudi Arabian sovereign wealth fund PIF's tower injures 11

Yemen's Iran-backed Houthis hit Saudi Arabian sovereign wealth fund Public Investment Fund Tower on Tuesday, injuring 11.

The kingdom's civil defense directorate did not name the tower, but posted an image showing damage to the PIF tower. Eyewitness reports by Al Jazeera also indicated that the building that was hit was the PIF Tower in Riyadh.

The injured included two Saudi citizens, two Indians, three Nepalis, and four Egyptians.

The attack comes as tensions have flared up again between Saudi Arabia, which is leading an international coalition to restore the internationally recognized government of Yemen after it was forced to flee in 2015, and the Houthis.

Turki al-Maliki, a spokesperson for the coalition, said one of the towers of the financial center was targeted in a "cowardly terrorist attack," referring to the financial center in the Saudi capital of Riyadh.

He added that authorities were conducting an on-site investigation at the scene of the attack.

This has been the second attack by the Iran-backed group in six days on the tower, although the previous attack did not cause any casualties.

On Wednesday, Houthi brigadier general Yahya Saree warned on X that the port of Jeddah and all Saudi ports along the Red Sea coast will become "targets for our forces," if Riyadh attacks the Yemeni Red Sea port of Hodeidah.

The coalition had asked civilians to evacuate the port "for their safety," on Nov. 20.

Attacks by the rebel Houthis have been escalating on Saudi Arabia, with the group targeting one of Saudi Arabia's oil refineries on Sunday, according to Reuters. The Houthis also launched drone and ballistic missiles against the country's airports, most recently targeting the King Khalid International Airport on Oct. 25, according to reports.

The new attacks come amid wider strikes by Tehran and Iran-linked forces in the Gulf, with Bahrain also reporting that it was attacked by Iran over the weekend. Bahrain is home to the U.S. Navy's Fifth Fleet.

"As a result of the heinous Iranian aggression, a civilian industrial facility was targeted, causing a fire at the site," Bahrain's Ministry of Interior said on X.

The ministry later said two people, a Bahraini citizen and a Pakistani resident, were injured in the attack.

— CNBC's Azhar Sukri contributed to this report.

#2828. Agencies announce dollar thresholds for smaller loan exemption from appraisal requirements for higher-priced mortgage loans

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 3:00 p.m. EST Share

The Consumer Financial Protection Bureau, the Federal Reserve Board, and the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency today announced that the 2027 threshold for higher-priced mortgage loans that are subject to special appraisal requirements will increase from $34,200 to $35,500.

The threshold amount will be effective January 1, 2027, and is based on the 3.9 percent annual percentage increase in the Consumer Price Index for Urban Wage Earners and Clerical Workers, known as CPI-W, as of June 1, 2026.

The Dodd-Frank Act added special appraisal requirements for higher-priced mortgage loans to the Truth in Lending Act, including that creditors obtain a written appraisal based on a physical visit to the interior of the home before making a higher-priced mortgage loan. The rules implementing these requirements contain an exemption for loans at or below a threshold amount that is adjusted annually to reflect CPI-W increases.

#2826. Agencies announce dollar thresholds for applicability of truth in lending and consumer leasing rules for consumer credit and lease transactions

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 3:00 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Reserve Board and the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau today announced the dollar thresholds used to determine whether certain consumer credit and lease transactions in 2027 are subject to certain protections under Regulation Z (Truth in Lending) and Regulation M (Consumer Leasing).

By law, the agencies are required to adjust the thresholds annually based on the annual percentage increase in the Consumer Price Index for Urban Wage Earners and Clerical Workers, known as CPI-W. Transactions at or below the thresholds are subject to the protections of the regulations.

Specifically, based on the 3.9 percent annual percentage increase in the CPI-W as of June 1, 2026, Regulation Z and Regulation M generally will apply to consumer credit transactions and consumer leases of $76,300 or less in 2027. However, private education loans and loans secured by real property, such as mortgages, are subject to Regulation Z regardless of the amount of the loan.

Federal Register notice: Consumer Leasing (Regulation M) (PDF)

Federal Register notice: Truth in Lending (Regulation Z) (PDF)

#2825. There are 5,100 newly minted Anthropic millionaires — here’s all the stuff they don’t tell you about when you suddenly get rich

Things are about to get very weird for the thousands of employees of Anthropic who just became, or soon will become, multimillionaires.

Anthropic's IPO on Nov. 19 turned around 5,100 nonfounder "Ants," as the company's staffers are known, into "paper millionaires," and at least 400 will be worth north of $100 million.

Few of us will ever make a million-dollar windfall from an employer. And fewer still will win the lottery or magically land a massive sum from an estranged rich uncle. But don't be too jealous of these employees' gains. Sure, money can buy a lot — and it would be hard to say no if you do strike it rich — but there are a remarkable number of downsides to sudden wealth.

And they may explain why you likely won't see all of Anthropic's newly minted millionaire employees heading for the exits.

First of all, these new moguls won't be able to access all their wealth immediately. The terms of the stock sale outlined in Anthropic's revised S-1 allow employees to cash out up to 10% of the value of their holdings on the first day of trading following the date of the prospectus, but the rest can only be exercised after various vesting periods pass or lock-up terms are achieved, based on the individual's agreements with the company.

Some won't be able to realize all their gains for years. There are many financial institutions willing to lend based on paper gains, or to buy options — but their wealth will also continue to fluctuate with the rise and fall of the company's stock price.

Even after those riches become liquid, research shows that only a small but still significant percentage of employees will leave as a direct result of their new wealth. No, they aren't jetting off to the South Pacific to board their new yacht — which is what I would do in my "I just got $10 million" fantasy. Instead, according to a 2017 study from the Federal Reserve, an estimated 17.5% of employees at a newly public company will use their newfound wealth as a financial cushion to join another pre-IPO startup or form another company.

These departures, the study finds, may be particularly painful for Anthropic since the most valuable and entrepreneurially minded employees tend to bolt for this reason.

Experts who deal with suddenly wealthy employees expect that many will keep on working because they love it. "They're successful people in their own right," said David Rust, a financial advisor and co-founder of wealth management firm Quartz Financial in Austin, Texas.

Rust said that of his firm's high-tech employee clients who get sudden IPO wealth, "very few just go play golf every day. They want to stay busy and active, and a lot of them will scale back depending on their age, but very few of them say, 'OK, I'm done with that.'"

Others will keep working because their payout wasn't life-changing, said Susan Bradley, a certified financial planner and founder of The Sudden Money Institute, a Palm Beach Gardens, Florida-based financial advisory firm that specializes in clients undergoing sudden wealth events.

That's especially true for those whose millions measure in the low single digits. "You can go through $2 million pretty quickly in the world we live in," said Bradley.

A sum north of $1 million might seem life-changing to most of America, but it's chump change in Anthropic's headquarters city of San Francisco, the fifth most expensive city in the United States, with nearby San Jose coming in at third, according to a Q3 2026 Cost of Living report from Council on Community and Economic Research, which tracks metro- and state-level economic data.

In 2025, the Bay Area had the second-highest number of people with a net worth of $100 million or more of any major metro in the world, according to a study by New World Wealth, a market research firm, in conjunction with Henley & Partners, a global residency and citizenship planning firm. That ranking is sure to get a boost from Anthropic's IPO.

Those who stay at Anthropic may be in for a bumpy ride. A study published in July in Management Science by two researchers from the University of Oklahoma found that employee satisfaction generally tends to crater after an IPO, especially among the earliest employees who had the biggest IPO payday.

The study found that the primary reason for this dissatisfaction is that the rules change when a company is public, and the free-wheeling startup energy, decision-making and risk-taking can be dampened by shareholders and SEC rules.

Plus, imagine trying to get an employee base of 96% newly minted millionaires to continue to work super-duper hard. That's bound to cause some intracompany friction, especially if you are a manager who didn't get a big payout.

Another important matter that I don't game out in my $10 million fantasy, and the Anthropic millionaires probably didn't either, is how strange their friends, family and significant others are about to get. What would you do if your husband, girlfriend, sister or best friend was suddenly the richest person you'd ever met in your life?

Those relationships are going to change and not necessarily for the better, said Paul Hokemeyer, a licensed marriage and family therapist and author of "Fragile Power: Why Having Everything is Never Enough." Think of all the times the new millionaires are going to face someone with their hand out, many of whom will be related to them by blood or marriage.

"In my practice, I see a dynamic where the person of wealth gives out of a place of generosity," said Hokemeyer, "and in short order their acts of largesse create unhealthy dependencies and entitlement. Then, when the giver decides to stop or pull back, the recipient becomes a victim and the giver a perpetrator." He advises his clients to make no personal or charitable financial gifts for six months to a year, and not until they've created a specific giving plan.

In general, what do experts say is the best thing these new millionaires can do do to deal with all the weirdness that's about to come their way? Absolutely nothing.

Work with professionals to address the urgent legal, tax and financial matters, of course, but don't peacock, don't buy stuff, don't move, don't quit, don't believe for a moment that you are suddenly super hot based on all the new attention you're getting from the opposite sex. It's all just noise.

Bradley suggests taking a break from any decisions that don't need to be made. "Even if it's as simple as saying, 'This is big. It's a little overwhelming right now, a little more than I expected, and I'm going to take my time.' Just buy some space."

Fortunately, $10 million can buy a lot of space.

Kris Frieswick is a columnist, journalist and humorist who covers personal finance, wealth, travel, real estate, luxury, and the strange intersection of business and culture. Her work has appeared in The Wall Street Journal, Vanity Fair, Inc. Magazine, CFO Magazine, Entrepreneur, Departures, Men's Journal, Boston Magazine, The Boston Globe and a wide variety of national and regional publications. Her debut novel is The Ghost Manuscript, a contemporary thriller with a historical backbone, and her humor can be found in The Fresh Hell Newsletter on Substack. She was previously an editor at the Wall Street Journal, Inc. Magazine and CFO magazine. She is an avid long-distance cyclist and traveler.

#2823. Mavericks vs Nuggets Recap: 3 things from the Mavericks 120-101 win against the Nuggets

The Dallas Mavericks picked up their most impressive win of the season Tuesday night, defeating the Denver Nuggets 120-101 in Denver. This was an NBA cup group game, and the win kept the Mavericks undefeated in group play.

This was a dominant showing from the Mavericks in one of the hardest road environments in the NBA. Dallas led nearly the entire game, taking the lead with 7:30 left in the first quarter and never letting Denver get back on top.

Dallas' defense was the star of the first half, limiting the Nuggets to 39 points, the Nuggets worst first-half scoring mark of the season. In the first quarter both teams started a bit cold, but in the second quarter the Mavericks defense really took over. Dallas' swarming, aggressive style flummoxed a Nuggets team that wanted to slow the game down in the half-court. The Mavericks played the passing lanes well, with nine first-half steals, leading to multiple transition opportunities.

In the second half the Nuggets were more successful in making this more a half-court game, but Dallas answered every single rally attempt. Denver closed the gap to as little as three in the third quarter, but would never get it closer than that. Kyrie Irving woke up in the second half, and started a run to start the fourth quarter that truly put the game away. This was a balanced game from the Mavericks, as while Irving and Cooper Flagg had their highlight plays, they struggled overall with their shot.

This is a landmark win for a Dallas team on the rise. Let's go over our takeaways.

Dallas still has a pretty funky roster, imbalanced with so many bigs and forwards and weak on guards. So much of that talk in the summer and preseason reflected more on what that imbalance would mean on offense, but it was just as important to talk about what that meant for the Mavericks defense. Dallas doesn't have a lockdown perimeter guy — a point-of-attack defender, as the kids say. So how do you overcome that in a league dominated by elite perimeter ball handlers and high-tempo, spread pick-and-roll play? The answer: use all those athletic wings and bigs to play like absolute mad men.

Credit to coach Dusty May and his staff, they have imbued a defensive identity on this team that it has sorely lacked over the last few seasons. Dallas might not have defensive-stopper archetypes, but they have so many rangey wings and bigs — Cooper Flagg, PJ Washington, Zaccharie Risacher, Caleb Martin, Naji Marshall, Daniel Gafford, and Morez Johnson Jr. Those athletes have been unleashed, playing up and shooting the passing lanes to cause havoc. It hasn't always worked, and we've seen teams exploit this aggressive defense with spacing, shooting, and off-ball movement. But boy, when it works, and when the Mavericks are locked in with their aggression and rotating well, it's awfully impressive to watch. The Mavericks had the Nuggets in absolute hell in that second quarter where Dallas outscored Denver 34-22.

Dallas had 13 steals tonight, scoring 26 points off the 18 Denver turnovers. This also led to the Mavericks having an overwhelming 35-7 fastbreak points advantage and an absolutely preposterous 68-38 points in the paint advantage. Dallas made Denver look old and slow. Kudos to this coaching staff and a roster of players that have absolutely bought in.

Morez Johnson Jr.'s rookie season has been rough to say the least, with lower-body injuries during the summer and preseason seemingly zapping all of explosiveness. We've seen glimpses of Johnson's talents at various points of this young season, but they've been few and far between. Against the Nuggets Tuesday night, Johnson showed off why he was a top-10 pick this past summer.

Johnson finished with nine points, 11 rebounds, and a steal in 19 points. He led the Mavericks in rebounds despite being eighth in minutes played. He held his own against Nikola Jokic, keeping Jokic in check during the second half when the Nuggets were furiously trying to rally. Johnson punctuated this effort with a great dunk right at the end of the third quarter, showing some leaping ability that just hasn't been there for most of the season. Johnson also banked in a three-pointer, which was also fun and funny.

With Daniel Gafford unable to grab any defensive rebounds (only four in 27 minutes), Dallas is desperate for any rebounding help. Hopefully Johnson can build off this performance.

Kyrie Irving's final stat line doesn't seem great — 11 points on 5-of-19 shooting, and only 1-of-8 from threes. If you didn't watch the game, you'd think it was one of Irving's worst. And weirdly it both was and wasn't.

The shooting was as bad as it looked for spurts of the first half, but Irving willed himself to some big buckets in the second half and had some spectacular assists in the third quarter. Also Irving continued his off-ball brilliance, moving well off the ball and creating opportunities for himself and his teammates.

If you have a chance, rewatch the second half and focus on Irving. Whenever he doesn't have the ball, he's almost constantly on the move. Obviously there are times when Irving is off the ball and standing in the corner, but his constant motion without the ball causes so much trouble for opposing defenses. Irving's cuts toward the basket seemingly get him either a layup or free throw attempts almost every time, and that happened again against the Nuggets.

It's rare you see someone of Irving's talent be so comfortable and useful as an off-ball player. Irving relishes the dirty work of making cuts and moving, knowing that movement doesn't always directly benefit him. But it benefits his teammates, and it's clear Irving cares. It's just nice to watch, and this performance was another example of that.

#2822. NFL predictions: 4 keys to a Lions victory over the Bears in Week 12 on Thanksgiving

The Detroit Lions (8-2) and Chicago Bears (8-2) will face off at Ford Field in Detroit in a Week 12 showdown on Thanksgiving Day. With former Lions offensive coordinator Ben Johnson calling the shots in Chicago, it's no surprise they mirror Detroit's offense in several ways, and their recent success has certainly earned Lions coach Dan Campbell's respect.

"They're a lot like us. They play on the hunt, and they're aggressive and run the football. And so this is a heck of a matchup. We're looking forward to it."

As strong as the Bears team has been over the past season and a half, going 15-2 against non-NFC North opponents, they've struggled against their rivals, producing a 2-6 record against the Lions, Packers, and Vikings. Johnson hasn't beaten the Lions since leaving Detroit, and the last time he was at Ford Field, the Lions took it to him, winning 52-21. Expect Johnson to be motivated to change the narrative here.

This week's prediction preview is sponsored by the folks at FanDuel Sportsbook, who currently have the Lions listed at +1900 to win this year's Super Bowl and are currently 2.5-point underdogs in Week 12. Let's take a closer look at this matchup and predict how the Lions will beat the Bucs in this week's Honolulu Blueprint.

For a detailed look at the opposing scheme, check out our complementary breakdown piece: Lions Week 12 preview: Breaking down the Bears' offensive and defensive schemes.

Looking at the box score statistics, the Bears defense has some impressive numbers: 19.7 points allowed (6th best); 310.5 yards allowed per game (7th); and EPA per play: -0.15 (7th). But when you look closer at the efficiency rates, those numbers lose their luster. For example, their per-play numbers are in the bottom 10 in the NFL: EPA per play: 5.7 (28th); per pass: 7.7 (24th); and per run: 4.5 (25th).

The large disparity is mostly because the Bears' defense has played the fewest snaps in the NFL (542). Chicago has been able to keep their defense on the sideline for two reasons: 1) Their offense holds the ball a league-leading 56.86% of the time; and 2) their defense gets off the field on 3rd down, allowing just a 26.6% conversion rate (Best in the NFL) and a third-down EPA allowed of -0.49 (2nd).

In the Bears' most recent loss, the Packers succeeded in both categories, winning time of possession (30:24) and converting 4 of 12 (33%) on third down—and 1 of 1 on 4th down—though the real difference came by converting third downs in critical situations, earning a third-down EPA of +0.25.

The Lions' success on third down started off slow in 2026 but has steadily improved. Over their six-game winning streak, the Lions' third-down success rate has ranged from 37-39%, but entering this game, it's risen to nearly 42% for the season, after converting 7 of 11 (64%) with a +0.29 EPA on third down against the Bucs' stout defense last week.

On third down this season, the Lions mainly lean on Amon-Ra St. Brown (38% target share), Jameson Williams (17%), and Jahmyr Gibbs (17%) in the passing game. Here's a look at how each has performed on third downs this season:

St. Brown: 18 receptions on 31 targets for 243 yards (13.5 per) and 0.27 EPA per third-down target

Williams: 8 receptions on 14 targets for 145 yards (18.13 per) and 0.37 EPA per third-down target

Gibbs: 11 receptions on 14 targets for 114 yards (10.36 per) and 0.29 EPA per third-down target

One of the main ways the Lions will try to maintain possession is by establishing the run early. Jahmyr Gibbs currently leads the NFL with 1,098 rushing yards and 13 rushing touchdowns, plus 1,502 yards from scrimmage and 15 total touchdowns.

Again, the Bears' box-score numbers make this look like a tough matchup. They are putting up top-10 numbers against the run: 94 run yards allowed per game (7th), a 39.6% success rate (6th), -0.1 EPA per rush allowed (8th), and 68 missed tackles (5th fewest in the NFL).

However, the Bears' defensive efficiency numbers are once again on the other end of the spectrum, as they're allowing 4.5 yards per rush (25th), 1.69 yards before contact (27th), and a 13.4% missed-tackle rate (25th); volume has been their biggest asset, and it could be their biggest enemy.

If the Bears don't improve their tackle rate, Gibbs could give them problems all day. Here's Next Gen Stats with more on Gibbs ' success in forcing missed tackles:

Jahmyr Gibbs has forced a league-high 92 missed tackles this season, 24 more than the next-closest player, Kenneth Walker (68). Those missed tackles have led to a league-high 496 yards after missed tackles, over 100 more than Jaylen Warren (388). That production has come consistently, as he has forced 10 or more missed tackles in four different games, the most in the NFL. The Bears have missed 13.4% of their tackle opportunities this season, the eighth-highest rate in the NFL.

Since the Bears acquired All-Pro defensive tackle Jeffrey Simmons, teams have slightly adjusted how they attack the Bears in the run game. The Seahawks, who faced Chicago in Simmons' first game, ran inside on 53% of plays and were held below their season rushing average. The next week, the Bucs stayed away from inside runs (43%) and instead attacked the edge 57% of the time, rushing for 126 yards. Last Sunday, the Saints followed the Bucs' strategy, attacked the edge on 55% of runs, and gained 120 yards on the ground (27 yards above their league average)

Look for the Lions to do the same. This season, the Lions attack the edge with outside runs on 54.6% of run plays (9th-highest rate), mainly leaning on an outside zone blocking scheme. According to Next Gen Stats, in these situations, Gibbs has gained 500 yards rushing, including 17 explosive run plays (best in the NFL), and has forced 43 missed tackles (best in the NFL).

While offenses have adjusted their approach with Simmons in the mix for the Bears, Chicago has adjusted its defense as well.

The Bears defensive coordinator, Dennis Allen, has historically run heavy man-coverage concepts (38-40% of the time), and while they started the season at that pace, they've since scaled things back and leaned more on zone concepts. Since the acquisition of Simmons, this has shifted even more, and over the last three games, the Bears have only run man coverage 22% of the time, while also leaning into more nickel sets (increasing from 59% to 70%) and two-high safety shells (up from 52% to 62%).

Essentially, with Simmons in the fold, the Bears are leaning into more four-man fronts and dropping seven into coverage to prevent big passing plays, and so far the results have been positive. Over the last three games, they've only allowed explosive plays on 5.2% of passing plays, the second-best mark in the NFL.

For the Lions passing offense to find success, they'll need to do two things: 1) expose coverage with pre-snap diagnosis, and 2) be patient and take advantage of what the Bears are giving them.

Lions quarterback Jared Goff is one of the best in the NFL at reading defenses pre- and post-snap. He's highly successful throwing against both man (EPA +0.52, 1st) and zone (+0.19, 8th) coverages, and has the skill players available to beat both.

St. Brown is Goff's default option on most downs, especially on short and intermediate routes and in critical moments. In last year's matchups, St. Brown dominated his one-on-ones against the Bears, catching 9 of 11 targets for 115 yards and 3 touchdowns in their first meeting, then securing 11 of 15 for 139 yards in their second.

But the Lions should also have an advantage deep, where Williams has been thriving lately. This season, when throwing deep, Goff has targeted Williams on half his attempts (12 attempts) and connected with him 67% of the time (8 receptions). According to Next Gen Stats, the Bears are allowing "45.7% completion percentage (7th-highest), 649 yards (2nd-most) and six touchdowns (tied-most) on deep passes this season."

In last season's matchups, Williams caught 2 of 4 targets for 108 yards and a touchdown, then followed it up by securing 6 of 8 targets for 74 yards in their second meeting. Expect another few deep shots in this game.

While Caleb Williams will get all the national attention in this game, he's been in a bit of a rut of late. Over the last three games, Williams has thrown for fewer than eight yards per pass attempt, along with five touchdowns and five interceptions. In the one game he exceeded 250 yards passing, his passer rating was just 61.5 because of turnovers—four interceptions to the Bucs' defense.

With Williams' play a bit erratic, look for the Lions to work hard to limit the Bears' rushing attack. And like on defense, the box score numbers look a lot better than their efficiency stats due to volume:

Rush yards per attempt: 4.2 (14th); EPA per rush: -0.06 (16th)

Explosive runs over last four games: 5.3% (31st in the NFL)

Runs of 10+ yards: 29 on season (13th) — only six in the last four games

Success rate over last four games: 40.8% (16th)

If the Lions can slow down the Bears' run game, Williams tends to try to force the issue. Williams is a dynamic athlete with an uncanny ability to extend plays and keep his options open, but he has tended—especially over the last three weeks—to try and force big plays instead of taking easier checkdowns, leading to mistakes and turnovers.

Last Sunday, after the Lions beat the Bucs, Kerby Joseph said he was disappointed he didn't secure an interception on his return because Baker Mayfield wasn't willing to test them deep. The Lions won't have that problem with Williams, so Joseph will likely get an opportunity to get his hands on one in this game.

#2820. Budget carrier Norse Atlantic falls 78% after warning its finances are ‘critical’ amid soaring fuel costs

Norse Atlantic shares plummeted Wednesday after it revealed the company is in a "critical financial situation" as soaring fuel costs amid the Iran war strain budget carriers during the winter season.

The Norwegian low-cost carrier said its situation is dependent on securing financing for its short-term liquidity needs.

The company has had discussions about a potential strategic takeover as well as other options including ACMI contracts, which refer to short-term capacity leasing.

"Norse is also engaged in discussions with selected parties on short term financing, however, there can be no assurances that such discussions will result in a viable solution," it added.

The start of the U.S.-Iran war on Feb. 28 sent jet fuel prices soaring, putting pressure on airlines around the world. Prices are now up nearly 72% to $155 per barrel year-on-year as of the week ending Nov. 20, according to the International Air Transport Association's Jet Fuel Price Monitor.

Norse, which offers cheap long-haul flights between Europe, North America, Africa, and Asia, said in October that it was forced to fly with reduced capacity that month, due to "prolonged high fuel prices."

Norse Atlantic's CEO Eivind Roald told CNBC in May that he was not "overly concerned" about access to fuel and said the airline didn't have insight that the crisis would have an immediate impact.

"Fuel price rose by more than 100% and the beginning of March we had already sold most of our seats for March so it was difficult to overcompensate with higher prices for those few seats left," Roald said.

The airline had cancelled all flights to and from Los Angeles at the time due to the cost of jet fuel, impacting over 150,000 passengers. Roald also said Norse cancelled more than 700 flights in total, which impacted more than 240,000 passengers.

The company said in August that geopolitical disruptions and higher jet fuel costs hit profitability in the first half of the year. Higher fuel costs, lower aircraft use, and operational disruptions led to an $8.4 million second-quarter loss, measured before interest, taxes, depreciation, amortization, and rent.

EasyJet, another low-cost airline impacted by high fuel costs, reported Wednesday that profit before tax slumped 89% to £71 million ($92.7 million) for the year ending in September 2026. Revenue growth was unable to offset the impact of an 11% increase in fuel costs due to the Middle East conflict, the company said.

Earlier this year, Ryanair's CEO Michael O'Leary told CNBC that other European carriers would struggle if jet fuel prices continued to soar. "I think there will be failures."

"If it continues at $150 a barrel into July, August, September, then you'll see European airlines fail and that, in the medium term, would probably be good for Ryanair's business."

Meanwhile, Ryanair's chief financial officer Neil Sorahan told CNBC in May: "I think we will see some of the weaker carriers who were already struggling before the war possibly go to the wall in the winter."

Several smaller European airlines have stopped flying this winter. Slovakia's AirExplore ceased operations, Ireland's Fly4 entered provisional liquidation, and Spain's AlbaStar suspended flights while seeking creditor protection.

Spain's Plus Ultra has also suspended all flights while it restructures, though it has not confirmed a permanent closure.

#2819. Is a bottom in for the Nasdaq-100? Charts to watch

(See the video above for Frank's complete chart breakdown.)

Is the Nasdaq-100's recent bounce a sign that the bottom is in? We're going to look at several charts to help answer that question.

First, let's remember how we got here. In September, the NDX broke out from a bullish cup-and-handle pattern, producing a 33,200-upside target. That breakout quickly failed, however, and gave way to a bearish pattern that broke down aggressively two weeks ago.

The decline brought the index relatively close to its 26,350 downside target before the current rebound began.

Zooming out, it's telling to see where the bounce originated. Three important forms of support converged in the 25,000-26,000 area:

This confluence of support helped encourage buying after a roughly 15% decline.

For perspective, we also see what larger drawdowns of 20%, 30%, 40%, and 50% would look like … a lot worse.

The following chart tracks NDX drawdowns from 52-week highs going back to just after the financial crisis. Only a handful of declines exceeded 20%, and three produced relatively quick reversals: late 2018, the Covid lows in 2020 and April 2025.

Historically, buying into declines of approximately 15%–25% has generally paid off, even when additional downside occurred first (yellow highlights).

The chart also highlights previous declines of 10%–16% in green, putting the current pullback into perspective.

The major exception was 2022, when the NDX eventually declined nearly 36% over approximately 10 months ... a much worse outcome, which many investors are concerned could happen again now.

That raises the key question: Are we experiencing another routine correction, or the early stages of a larger bear market?

One important clue will be how far this bounce progresses.

Any rally that fails to reclaim the previous highs could establish a significant lower high. Additional lower highs would provide stronger evidence of a developing, longer downtrend.

We saw this in March 2022, when that first sharp rebound effort failed to reach the previous high before a much more severe decline followed.

The bottom line: The current bounce is encouraging, particularly given where it originated and knowing how well buying prior 10-20% drawdowns has paid off.

From here, it's all about the follow through.  We don't need to see new highs right away, but any rally from here that's met with another wave of strong selling could reignite downside momentum.

Frank Cappelleri is the founder and president of CappThesis, an independent research firm that helps active investors through time-tested chart and statistical analysis. Prior to starting CappThesis, he spent 25 years on Wall Street as an equity sales trader, technical analyst, research sales specialist and desk strategist.

#2816. Trump wanted Jensen Huang back. Now, the president is all alone in Washington

President Donald Trump wanted Jensen Huang back at the helm of Nvidia. Now that the chip mogul is reinstated, Trump is cheering alone in Washington.

Trump celebrated and claimed credit after Huang was reinstated by the Nvidia board on Monday, a stunning return to power for the embattled CEO. In a nearly hourlong phone call with CNBC, the president went as far as saying he "made the deal" to bring Huang back.

But Trump's jubilation stands in stark contrast to the reaction from even his staunchest allies on Capitol Hill, who recoiled after the reinstatement and vowed to continue their investigations into Huang — whose relationship with a potential Chinese intelligence asset they fear may have jeopardized U.S. national security.

"The risk remains that there could be information that a Chinese spy knows about a key individual in the supply chain, and that's not worth the risk," Sen. Kevin Cramer, R-N.D., said Tuesday on CNBC's "Squawk Box." "We might even have to put a babysitter on Jensen Huang for crying out loud ... just to make sure that he doesn't do something that's going to risk the United States."

Trump's rare isolation from his party over Huang comes after the GOP was throttled in the midterm elections, as Democrats rode a blue tsunami to capture the Senate and the largest House majority this century. And it suggests Republicans, who have spent a decade in lockstep with the president, may have found an issue on which they can't bring themselves to take Trump's side.

"I can accept what the president has done," Cramer said. "But we have to put guardrails around to protect the United States of America."

Many midterm voters identified Silicon Valley's most visible outputs, artificial intelligence and data centers, as top concerns motivating them to cast ballots for Democrats. The CNBC All-America Economic Survey in October found 63% of registered voters oppose an AI data center within 10 miles of their home, while just 13% support one.

By timing his extraordinary intervention in Nvidia so soon after the GOP's historic losses, Trump could spark a national debate about corporate governance and responsibility, former Under Secretary of State Richard Stengel said.

"With the repudiation of Trumpism in the midterms and voter revulsion with corruption, I think there will be a turn back to a more value-based duty of care for boards of directors and away from the current maximization of shareholder value," Stengel told CNBC in an email.

"Directors are citizens first, shareholders second. The idea that you put profit above national security should disgust shareholders, not please them," Stengel, who worked in the Obama administration, wrote.

Bipartisan lawmakers are now racing to pass a series of export control bills before the year ends, a call that has only grown louder since Huang was reinstated.

There's "not just frustration or political worry, but actual, substantive national security concern here from Democrats and Republicans," Chris McGuire, senior fellow for China and emerging technologies at the Council on Foreign Relations, told CNBC.

"This has historically been a bipartisan issue," McGuire said. "Democrats and Republicans generally pretty much agree that we shouldn't be sending our most advanced chips to China."

"If it happened in massive quantities, people want to know why, how, and they want to make sure that it doesn't happen again," he said. "And putting your thumb on the scale in this clear of a way seems like it could undercut those efforts."

The White House didn't immediately respond to a request for comment on the political dynamics of Trump's involvement in reinstating Huang.

Republicans will have the first crack at investigating Huang next week before ceding power to Democrats in January. Democrats have vowed their own investigations into the Nvidia CEO and his ties to the president and his Cabinet. The Democrats who will lead those investigations are now hopeful that Republicans will join them.

"I've been seeing a lot of Republicans outraged and scandalized by what has just happened," House Judiciary Committee ranking member Jamie Raskin, D-Md., said Tuesday on "Squawk Box," when asked if Republicans may support a bipartisan investigation of Nvidia.

"When else in American history can you think of a time when a president of the United States called up a corporate board of directors meeting and then essentially dictated to them who was going to be their new CEO right after they had fired the guy?"

Trump throughout his second term has stated repeatedly that achieving AI dominance is required for the U.S. to maintain global superiority. His administration has championed the rapid growth of the country's tech giants and forged close ties with its leaders, especially Huang, while removing constraints on the industry and opposing some new regulatory efforts.

At the same time, Trump has defended Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick against accusations of slow-walking export controls on China, the fiercest competitor in the AI race. And while Trump recently discussed AI during an in-person meeting with Chinese President Xi Jinping, he appeared to deliver few demands and achieved few assurances about Beijing's efforts to acquire U.S. chips.

"To say we can't do AI safety because of China, and then also be actively loosening the controls on China, makes no sense," McGuire said.

Some of Trump's fiercest allies have raised similar qualms.

"Reinstating Jensen Huang is going to blow back on our national security," MAGA activist Laura Loomer wrote in an X post Monday. "Eventually there has to be accountability for jeopardizing US national security. China didn't just get access to Nvidia chips by accident."

Newt Gingrich, the influential former Republican speaker of the House, in an X post after Huang's reinstatement, wrote, "Congress should really dig into this as a national security issue of enormous proportions and also a significant failure in the federal bureaucracy."

Their criticisms sharply contrast with the praise coming from Silicon Valley and some of the Trump-friendly voices within it, such as Elon Musk, who wrote Monday, "The mistake has been corrected."

But that's a shrinking political constituency, McGuire said.

"The tech-right perspective is very out of touch with where most of the Republican Party is on these issues, to say nothing of the Democratic Party," he said.

"Those positions are just not going to be sustainable for much longer," he added. "I think they're already becoming unsustainable."

#2810. Stats Rundown: 5 numbers to know from the Mavericks 120-101 win over the Nuggets

The Dallas Mavericks (11-7) took on the Denver Nuggets (10-6) in Emirates Cup action on Tuesday night. Carrying a three-game winning streak into Denver, Dallas continued the trend with a 120-101 victory.

The Mavericks were leading by 13 points with 1:16 remaining in the second quarter. Jamal Crawford, announcing on the Peacock broadcast, stated that the final minute-plus was crucial – the score could easily balloon to 17, or be cut to 8. The Mavericks reeled off six more as part of a 15-0 run to bump the lead to 19 points.

Perhaps the Mavericks had the release of Grand Theft Auto VI on their minds, as they were stealing everything in sight. Dallas had nine steals in the first half – five of which came from Caleb Martin – which they turned into 17 points. This was a textbook example of turning defense into offense. Turning the ball over limited the Nuggets' offense in a big way and the Mavericks doubled the damage by scoring on the other end.

Throughout the first half, the Mavericks' three-point defense left a bit to be desired, but they weren't badly hurt due in part to the Nuggets missing their attempts. The third quarter flipped that script in a significant way. Dallas' lack of close-outs often left shooters wide open and Denver shot themselves right back into the game. The Nuggets hit six threes in the frame, with Christian Braun alone hitting three in a row in under a minute.

The Mavericks showed a very well-balanced offense, which was critical on a night where both Cooper Flagg and Kyrie Irving struggled a bit with their shot. All five Mavericks starters – Irving, Flagg, Martin, Zaccharie Risacher and Daniel Gafford – were joined by P.J. Washington and Naji Marshall in scoring double figures. Morez Johnson, Jr. was a single point shy of joining the club.

Coming into the night, Flagg needed to connect on two three-pointers to achieve the rare milestone of scoring 1,500 or more points and hitting 100 or more three-pointers before turning 20-years-old. With only a single made three, he temporarily fell short of joining Kobe Bryant as the only teenagers in league history to achieve the feat. He has just under a month to get there, with his 20th birthday arriving on Dec. 21.

#2809. Crypto talent will return ‘when AI implodes,’ Arthur Hayes says

Startup talent that has concentrated in artificial intelligence will return to the crypto industry "when AI implodes," according to Arthur Hayes, chief investment officer at Maelstrom Fund.

"A lot of talent has gone into the AI space, so I definitely think there'll be a resurgence in good founder talent coming back to crypto," Hayes said in an online interview with CNBC on Tuesday.

He added that some founders may also return once they "recognize" that their products could eventually be "usurped by Anthropic or OpenAI."

The scale and capabilities of the largest frontier AI labs have made it difficult to "build a real AI business," Hayes said, arguing that "most" founders simply "raise a bunch of money and then light it on fire."

He expects the AI trade to face "serious headwinds" in the second half of 2027, when leading labs begin confronting sizable bills tied to compute leases and other non-cancelable commitments.

Anthropic expects about $451.6 billion in compute and infrastructure obligations through the end of 2030, with roughly $70.7 billion due by the end of September 2027, according to its prospectus.

"Do they have the money? We're going to find out," Hayes said.

Broader enthusiasm around the AI trade has also cooled amid questions about future infrastructure spending. Even exuberance from Jensen Huang's return as Nvidia CEO on Monday proved short-lived, with the shares falling 5.9% on Tuesday and Anthropic dropping below its IPO price four days after its debut.

The co-founder of now-shuttered crypto exchange BitMEX also thinks bitcoin could test $50,000 if the Nasdaq continues to fall. The cryptocurrency has shown little sign of recovery after dropping as low as $55,142.16 on Saturday — its lowest level since July.

Bitcoin hit an all-time high of $126,279.63 in October last year and traded as high as $97,915.90 in January this year.

It has now fallen for seven straight weeks — only the third such streak in its history — with crypto exchange Bitfinex attributing the weakness to equity-market sentiment rather than interest-rate expectations.

Hayes said he would like to see bitcoin rebound from around $55,000 even as the Nasdaq continues to fall. That divergence, he said, would suggest markets were shifting from a broad sell-off toward pricing in a "reflation" scenario, with the Federal Reserve eventually providing more liquidity.

Neither bitcoin nor gold has yet decoupled from the Nasdaq, with Hayes describing the current market moves as a "correlation-led liquidation."

"Stuff goes down, you sell crypto first because it's easy, and then you sell everything else," he said.

Hayes said a bitcoin rally could push up funding rates and futures-basis returns, increasing the income Ethena earns from its hedged crypto positions. That could make USDe, Ethena's synthetic dollar, more attractive and expand its circulation, he said, adding that he expects the ENA token to rise materially.

"Those are the two things that I'm keeping an eye on, and when I'm ready to pull the trigger on buying risk again, those will be the things we buy a lot of," Hayes said.

#2803. A trading strategy to ride out the Nvidia uncertainty and generate income in the process

Jensen Huang's abrupt resignation has sent shockwaves through the tech sector, dragging Nvidia's (NVDA) stock down roughly 30% since Oct. 6. The market despises uncertainty, and losing a visionary founder, particularly one without a well-telegraphed succession plan, makes it worse.

However, for opportunistic investors, this panic has made the stock cheaper (obviously) while options have become significantly more expensive. Trading in the $160s, Nvidia's valuation has taken a big haircut, yet its underlying business fundamentals remain largely intact. While Huang was the architect of Nvidia's AI dominance, the company's moat — superior GPUs, the CUDA software ecosystem, and networking expertise — does not walk out the door with him. The company's structural advantage in the global artificial intelligence rollout persists.

Buying good companies at a discount is investing 101, but the current market dynamics may do something else for those who use options.  Huang's departure guarantees that Nvidia will be operating under a cloud of transition for the foreseeable future. Executing a permanent CEO search while navigating global regulatory scrutiny takes time. Consequently, Nvidia's implied volatility (IV)—the market's pricing of expected future price swings — has spiked to highs not seen since the tariff tantrum in the first half of 2025, and this elevated IV is highly likely to persist for several months as the leadership drama slowly unfolds.

This prolonged period of high implied volatility can be a goldmine for option sellers, specifically those executing options overlays such as covered call strategies. When you buy NVDA shares at these depressed levels and simultaneously sell out-of-the-money call options against your position, the premiums you collect are directly tied to IV. Because fear and transition-related uncertainty have dramatically inflated these option prices, the income generated from selling covered calls is now significantly higher than usual.

This lowers the risk profile of holding the stock considerably over time. Consistently collecting these inflated premiums systematically lowers your overall cost basis in the underlying shares. If the stock trades sideways or recovers slowly during the CEO search, you are generating an outsized cash yield on a stagnant asset. If the stock experiences further downside turbulence, the heavy premium income acts as a substantial downside buffer, meaningfully reducing the strategy's overall risk over time.

By deploying a persistent covered call strategy, investors can leverage the exact fear driving the stock down to generate robust, risk-reducing income while maintaining exposure to the undeniable future of AI.

#2801. NBA Trade Rumors: Trade coming for the Jazz

#2798. Wealthy investors seek tax savings from municipal bonds as yields remain elevated

The business of tax-loss harvesting is booming with wealthy investors pouring billions of dollars into products like tax-aware long-short strategies. As 10-year Treasury yields have climbed to levels not seen since 2007, tax-savvy clients are also turning to municipal bonds to keep more of what they earn.

"High-net-worth investors, because of all the noise in direct indexing and tax-aware long-short strategies, have begun to expect tax management from all of their investments, including their fixed income," said Gavin Romm, senior vice president and head of separately managed accounts at AllianceBernstein.

While tax-loss harvesting can be done with other types of fixed income, high-income investors typically gravitate to municipal bonds for their tax-exempt income. Separately managed accounts, known as SMAs, held about $1.6 trillion in municipal bonds at the end of 2025, a 44% jump since 2017, per JPMorgan's estimate.

For top-bracket investors, municipal bonds can offer a twofold benefit: tax-equivalent yields that approach long-run stock-market returns, and losses that may offset gains elsewhere in a portfolio, according to Ran Leshem, BlackRock's head of SMA solutions. BlackRock, which manages $42 billion in muni SMAs, estimates that assets across muni SMAs have grown by 10% industry-wide over the past three years.

"You can get a tax-equivalent yield of over 7% that is becoming quite compelling for a lot of clients," said Leshem. "We are seeing yields that haven't been around for quite a few years, so it's a good opportunity for clients to lock in yields at these higher rates."

Rising yields earlier this fall pushed bond prices down, leaving some investors with paper losses, Leshem said. However, tax-loss harvesting can be harder with bonds than with stock, particularly municipal bonds, he noted. The municipal bond market is highly fragmented with more than 50,000 issuers, and it costs more and takes longer to transact, he said.

"There might not always be liquidity or someone willing to buy it for a fair price," Leshem said.

That said, bonds can offer a distinct tax advantage from equities, according to Romm.

By selling a stock at a loss, an investor can reduce the current tax bill by offsetting gains elsewhere. In exchange, the investor faces a potentially larger taxable gain if they later sell the replacement stock.

However, if an investor sells a bond at a loss and buys a replacement bond for more than its par value (or face value) the replacement bond's tax basis is generally adjusted down over time as it approaches maturity, Romm said. If the investor holds it to maturity, the amount repaid may match that adjusted basis, leaving no capital gain or loss on the replacement bond, he added.

"Tax-loss harvesting with equities, by definition, is tax deferral," he said. "With bonds, when done thoughtfully, it is actually tax avoidance."

That said, investors must be careful not to violate the IRS wash-sale rule by buying a "substantially identical asset" within 30 days before or after the sale.

That tax benefit gives fixed income another job beyond providing income and stability, Romm said.

"These aren't really positioned as get-rich strategies. They're more of stay-rich strategies," he said.

The Inside Wealth newsletter by Robert Frank is your weekly guide to high-net-worth investors and the industries that serve them.

#2793. Game Preview: San Antonio Spurs vs Portland Trail Blazers

After being stopped cold at 12 wins during their 1st win streak of the season, the Spurs have promptly started up another one, chugging right along even in the (one game) absence of their resident superstar.

Whether or not it continues will be decided by a challenging little three-game stretch against playoff teams, starting with last postseason's 1st round matchup, the Portland Trail Blazers.

The Spurs erupted from long-range against Portland in their first matchup this season, but past experience tells that these Blazers are far from pushovers, and are deeper than they were when they were giving the Spurs some degree of trouble in the playoffs. (Or, at least, were, until the injury bug bit them)

It seems unlikely that a Portland team ranking 11th in three-point percentage and 2nd in made threes will shoot 26% from downtown again, and the fact that this is an NBA Cup group stage may add a little zest to the quality of regular season effort in this one.

Still, the Spurs are 9-1 in their last 10 games, and if the Trail Blazers get absolutely manhandled on the boards again, they might not be able to get up enough shots anyway, having fallen eight threes shy of their season average as the Spurs held a 13 rebound advantage on the evening (6 of which were offensive rebounds).

Portland is perfectly capable of handling themselves in that arena, though, as a big team averaging just three rebounds less than the Spurs per game (which is, hilariously, the difference between 1st and 11th in rebounds).

One of the game's biggest questions will be the status of Victor Wembanyama, obviously, but De'Aaron Fox's status will also be a low-key point of interest (after being tripped by Jeremiah Fears against the Pelicans), as Portland's guard play is one of their most significant weaknesses.

Portland ranks in the bottom 20 in assists (22nd), turnovers given up (21st), turnover percentage (22nd), and assist-to-turnover ratio (22nd), all areas the Spurs rank inside the Top 5 in (3rd, 3rd, 3rd, and 1st).

If Wembanyama takes the court, this should be a win for the Spurs, barring another cold spell from three from San Antonio, or a scalding performance from Portland.

But even without Wembanyama, the Spurs might be able to wreak real havoc on the Trail Blazers' front-court with the presence of a healthy Fox.

Minus their availability, things might get a little sticky for the Spurs against a team deep enough for Jrue Holiday to be getting 6th Man of the Year consideration already. Though not if Stephon Castle has his say in the matter.

Here's hoping the Spurs get a little more heath luck than the bit they've already had.

Spurs Injuries: Victor Wembanyama: Questionable (Back), De'Aaron Fox: Questionable (Ankle), Tobias Harris: Out (Calf), Jayden Quaintance: Out (Knee), Dylan Harper: Out (Hip)

Trail Blazers Injuries: Ja Morant: Out (Leg), Shaedon Sharpe: Out (Knee), Chris Youngblood: Questionable (Heel), Vit Krejci: Out (Ankle)

#2790. Aaron Rodgers wishes he had a coach like Sean McVay

We have never seen a player, let alone a Hall of Fame quarterback, go off on a head coach while he's still on the team like what Aaron Rodgers did to Mike McCarthy on Wednesday. One day before the holiday, Rodgers made it clear that he's not thankful for McCarthy or the Pittsburgh Steelers after the quarterback was benched on Tuesday night and had to find out through teammate Pat Freiermuth. During a surprise appearance on The Pat McAfee Show on Wednesday, Rodgers said that McCarthy has been snoring during meetings, missing walkthroughs, and broke a halftime in the locker room during Sunday's loss to the Eagles with "1-2-3 Packers".

Rodgers said that he told McCarthy during a "contentious" meeting on Monday that he should do the right thing and give up play calling duties, so the head coach decided to bench him for Mason Rudolph for Friday's game against the Denver Broncos. The Steelers management and ownership decided to back McCarthy, according to Rodgers, so he says his career with the franchise is definitively over.

In the same interview, Rodgers name dropped Sean McVay, among a couple of other head coaches, as the types of play callers who probably get deeply involved with working with the starting quarterback to make a game plan and watch film, which he says McCarthy never does.

There were rumors floating around in previous years that if Matthew Stafford were to retire that Aaron Rodgers would be someone who may be interested in the organization and vice versa. It seems even more obvious now that what Rodgers has really wanted in a head coach is a play caller who seems invested in the game plan, someone like McVay. For all the grief that McVay gets when the Rams don't win the Super Bowl — and especially during a tumultuous season like this one — that there are qualities to being a head coach that never get appreciated.

Like being able to keep a powder keg from exploding. Clearly that's something that Mike Tomlin was doing for many years, and why Pittsburgh didn't fire him for so long despite not winning a playoff game in a decade.

If McVay is not the head coach next year, or the year after that, whether it's his choice or the franchise, remember how quickly things can fall apart without a reliable person to steady the ship. Even when the Steelers are 6-4, same record as the Rams, the boat can brake apart overnight.

#2787. Warriors feeling psychological boost of having injured Steph Curry around team

Warriors feeling psychological boost of having injured Steph Curry around team originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

SAN FRANCISCO – Warriors guard Brandon Williams scrimmaged Tuesday and is expected to return Wednesday against the Sacramento Kings after missing the last six games because of a mild left hamstring strain. Williams might as well have been listed with an illness, too.

Williams was injured in fewer than two minutes against the New Orleans Pelicans on Nov. 9. Steph Curry, in only 21 minutes, scored 25 points on 6-of-10 shooting, 4-of-7 from 3-point range and a perfect 9-of-9 from the free-throw line in a 138-87 win. The Warriors' 51-point beatdown put the cherry on top of a 6-0 homestand that extended their win streak to eight straight games.

The Warriors' perfect homestand ended on a Monday. The next Monday, in a game without injured Williams and Curry, the Warriors lost by 21 points in New Orleans to a Pelicans team without Trey Murphy III.

Curry grabbed at his right side in the opening minutes of the Warriors' five-game road trip in an eventual seven-point loss against the Houston Rockets. Curry was ruled out the next day for the rest of the road trip with a right abdominal oblique strain. His two-week re-evaluation is expected within the coming days.

Golden State lost its next four games by a combined 109 points.

Williams traveled with the team and watched the offensive struggle from the bench. The same can be said for his view of the Warriors scoring 88 points in their fifth straight game below 100 points, yet found a way to beat the Boston Celtics by one on Sunday night.

"Yeah, I ain't gonna lie, s–t's been tough watching that," Williams said Tuesday at Chase Center after practice.

Watching the eyesore reminded Williams of why he's here, and why he has been so valued early this season.

"I know exactly what I need to do. Just watching from afar, you see a little bit more," Williams said. "The offense gets stagnant at times, and I know that's what they brought me in here for – to keep it rolling, keep it going, keep everybody organized and stuff. So hopefully we see that next time on the court for sure."

Even with Curry, Williams was going to be missed. Without the two-time NBA MVP, Williams' importance has been amplified as a playmaker who has a scorer's mindset and can make defenses react to him from beating them off the dribble.

Williams scored 20 points when Curry missed his first game to rest this season. He's the Warriors' fourth-leading scorer at 10.4 points per game, but ranks second in points per 36 minutes (23.3).

The Warriors will take any offensive help they can find until Curry is back, and Williams is sure to bring life to a unit that has looked listless as of late.

While Williams will be an instant boost to the Warriors, Curry already was one against the Celtics, despite his injury.

Curry was at Warriors shootaround in San Antonio after his injury but flew home the same day hours before the game and missed the massive FAA flight interruptions. He's the sun in the Warriors' solar system, as coach Steve Kerr often says, and that includes emotionally as well.

The Warriors ached for Curry's return right when he went down. His presence was sorely missed outside of box score stats during the rest of a historically bad road trip, and it was again felt rooting from the sidelines Sunday night as the underdog Warriors upset the Celtics.

"Definitely helpful," Kerr said Tuesday. "I mean, he's just always a breath of fresh air. And the humor and the confidence that he has, you saw it the other night. Draymond [Green] hits a three from the corner, he's flying all over the place on the bench.

"There's just a different vibe when Steph is in the building, and it's good to have him here, even if he's not playing."

Curry got out of his chair and turned to the Warriors' bench in celebration when Green, hours after going at it all day on social media with former Sixth Man of the Year and 17-year NBA vet Eddie Johnson, opened the game with a three from the right corner.

Curry threw three fingers up in unison with teammates when Green drained his second three of the first quarter, again letting it fly from the right corner.

And when Curry's longest-tenured teammate made his third three from the right corner in the first six minutes of the game, he almost melted into his chair and fell onto the court.

Sunday's win that snapped the Warriors' five-game losing streak started with Green making threes, and ended with him stopping Jayson Tatum's game-winning attempt. Green and Kerr shared an extended, emotional hug. Curry waited behind for Green and embraced him as the two enjoyed another countless moment of their competitive spirits intertwined.

Rookie Yaxel Lendeborg needed Curry in every way during the Warriors' skid. He needed Curry's gravity that made the start of his career so smooth. He also needed his calming presence that inspires confidence.

Curry wasn't there when Lendeborg gave everyone an inside view into his mind and said he's "mentally f–ked up" after the Warriors lost by 38 points against the Spurs the game after losing their superstar. He wasn't there as Lendeborg hung his head in New Orleans, or when he looked lost in Dallas and Memphis.

But Curry was there Sunday in San Francisco sharing knowledge with Lendeborg, who liked a post about the moment on Instagram.

"Yeah, it's huge," Kerr said about Lendeborg being around Curry. "And I've said this, but I love the internal leadership we have on this team. Our vets have been fantastic. Draymond's leadership every day has been brilliant. Al and Steph are more the quiet leaders by example, but pull a guy aside and talk to him.

"Our veteran players have provided such great leadership for us this year, and it's really helpful, especially for the young guys, Yaxel in particular, given how much he's playing."

This is all new to Williams. His prior teams, the Portland Trail Blazers and Dallas Mavericks, saw stars like Damian Lillard, Anthony Davis, Kyrie Irving and others miss time due to injuries. Curry is different, and so is the culture he has built for the Warriors.

Having him on the bench and around the team is personally helpful to Williams, and Curry's backup point guard is impressed by the Warriors' resolve in recovering from being hit in the mouth.

"It helps a lot, specifically for me," Williams said. "Those teams that I've been on, like, Kyrie went down, the morale around the team just went down. It's the complete opposite case here. Everybody's the next man up. I think the thing out here, what is it, Strength In Numbers? I think that's a great motto to live by, and it's perfect for us right now.

"Everybody's got to step up, whether it's Gui [Santos], Will [Richard], Graham [Ike], [Malevy Leons], all of us got to step up. It's a collective group effort."

Narratives have run rampant since the Warriors' 2022 championship season. The numbers still tell an obvious story. The Warriors have gone 39-53, a 42.4 win percentage, without Curry in the regular season the last five years. They're 2-5 this season, and 2-6 if you include their loss in Houston.

Curry was in attendance for the Warriors' first loss without him this season, a hard-fought nine-point defeat in Phoenix on the second night of a back-to-back after playing the night before in San Francisco. He went wild on the sidelines during his second rest game as Lendeborg unleashed 37 points in a home win over the LA Clippers and could hardly contain himself, bringing energy to the bench as the Warriors got back in the win column against the Celtics.

Greatness is contagious, even if it's felt in different ways than usual.
