#2786. Berkshire breaks out to record high with fourth-quarter rally reaching 19%

(This is the Warren Buffett Watch newsletter, news and analysis on all things Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway. You can sign up here to receive it every Friday evening in your inbox.)

Berkshire Hathaway's red-hot shares are ending a strong week with four consecutive fresh record high closes.

Since the rally began with the start of the fourth quarter on October 1, the Class A shares have closed at record highs 11 times as they soared 19.1%.

The much more widely held B shares closed at new highs just 10 times but are also up 19.1% from their near-term low.

The gains are in sharp contrast to the S&P 500's 1.2% dip as investors appear to be seeing Berkshire as a relatively safe haven amid worries about AI stocks and spending.

Both Berkshire classes were up 3.9% on the week as the S&P advanced by 2.5%, its best week since early August.

Berkshire's B shares are now 7.5 percentage points ahead of the benchmark for the year so far.

After erasing their post-Buffett CEO announcement losses three weeks ago, Berkshire shares are now up almost 10% since last year's shareholders meeting.

They've gained around 29% since last August's lows and are up around 18% year-to-date.

Berkshire Hathaway is due to disclose its quarterly equity portfolio snapshot on Monday when it files its Q3 13F with the SEC.

We won't know for sure until then, but numbers in the company's third quarter 10-Q, released last Saturday, indicate Berkshire continued to reduce its Bank of America stake.

It would be the ninth consecutive quarter of selling.

As of June 30, Berkshire reported holding 483.4 million BAC shares valued now at $24.7 billion, it's fifth largest equity position.

On June 30, 2024, before the selling began, Berkshire owned just over 1 billion shares valued at $41.1 billion.

Since then, the number of shares in the portfolio has decreased by 53.2%, not including any Q3 sales, while the stock price has increased by 28.3%.

If there had been no reductions, the position would be worth $52.7 billion.

The belief Berkshire sold more BAC shares in the third quarter is based on a $372 million reduction in the cost basis for equities categorized by the company as "banks, insurance, and finance."

The 10-Q also reports $2.2 billion of pretax gains from equity sales.

Barron's notes Bank of America and American Express are the only financial stocks in the portfolio that have a cost basis low enough to generate a gain like that. Amex is one of the four stocks new CEO Greg Abel indicated would be held for "decades" to come in his February letter to shareholders.

In any case, Berkshire's almost 23% stake in Amex would have required it to quickly disclose any sales.

Based on the numbers in the 10-Q, the warrants Berkshire received as part of a 2011 $5 billion investment in the bank that it used in 2017 to buy 700 million shares at $7.14 each, and BAC's average stock price in the quarter, Barron's estimates 30 to 40 million shares may have been sold.

That would be roughly in line with the 30.2 million shares sold in the second quarter.

The case isn't as good, but Barron's thinks Berkshire may have bought more Alphabet shares in the period. That's based on a $2.3 billion increase in the cost basis for the category that includes Google's parent.

Overall, it looks like it will be a relatively quiet quarter with $2.4 billion in equity purchases and $2.8 billion in sales.

In the first quarter, Berkshire bought $15.9 billion and sold $24.1 billion of equities. In the second quarter, it purchased $23.5 billion and sold $3.7 billion.

All those numbers exclude the company's buybacks of its own stock.

In a term sheet filed early Friday, Berkshire Hathaway says it is selling more than 182 billion yen ($1.1 billion) worth of bonds with maturities between 2029 and 2056 and annual interest rates between 2.6% and 4.6%.

Bloomberg points out the rates, compared to benchmarks, are higher than its previous sale this year.

It quotes a fund manager as saying market sentiment "has become more sensitive," forcing issuers to make their offerings more attractive.

In April, Berkshire sold 272 billion yen ($1.7 billion) of bonds.

Berkshire uses the proceeds from the yen-denominated bonds to protect its ongoing investments in Japanese stocks from currency fluctuations.

Warren Buffett recommends Benjamin Graham's book, "The Intelligent Investor," and outlines what he considers Graham's three most important ideas.

WARREN BUFFETT: On investment books, if you're asking about that, I would recommend the first two books that Phil Fisher wrote back around 1960, "Common Sense [Stocks] and Uncommon Profits" and the second one ["Paths to Wealth Through Common Stocks"]. They're very good books.

You know, I obviously recommend, first and foremost, [Benjamin Graham's] "The Intelligent Investor," with chapters eight and 20 are the ones that you really should read.

Two of the — well, all of the important ideas in investing, really, are in that book, because there's only about three ideas. And those — two of them are emphasized in those two chapters...

One is that — to think of yourself — to think of investing as owning a business and not buying something that wiggles around in price.

And the second one is your attitude, which ties in with that, the attitude toward the market, that's covered in chapter eight. And if you have the proper attitude toward market movements, it's an enormous help in securities.

And the final chapter is on the margin of safety, which means, don't try and drive a 9,800-pound truck over a bridge that says it's, you know, "Capacity: 10,000 pounds." But go down the road a little bit and find one that says, "Capacity: 15,000 pounds."

Berkshire market capitalization: $1,259,897,054,755

Berkshire Cash as of Sept 30: $377.4 billion (Up 3.3% from June 30)

Excluding Rail Cash and Subtracting T-Bills Payable: $352.3 billion (Down 1.9% from June 30)

Berkshire repurchased $9.6 billion of its shares in Q3 2026.

Berkshire's top holdings of disclosed publicly traded stocks in the U.S. and Japan, by market value, based on the latest closing prices.

Holdings are as of June 30, 2026, as reported in Berkshire Hathaway's 13F filing on August 14, 2026, except for:

#2783. 10-year Treasury yield slides despite sticky inflation data as investors buy bonds for protection

U.S. Treasury yields fell on Wednesday, with investors fleeing to bonds for protection after October's personal consumption expenditures price index showed that inflation continued to accelerate.

The 10-year Treasury note yield — the main benchmark for mortgages, auto loans and credit card debt — was more than 3 basis points lower at 4.866%.

The yield on the 2-year Treasury note, which typically moves in line with short-term Federal Reserve interest rate decisions, was down more than 2 basis points at 4.65%.

The longer-dated 30-year Treasury yield, which tends to track geopolitical events, was more than 3 basis points lower at 5.254%.

One basis point equals 0.01%, or 1/100th of 1%, and yields and prices move inversely to one another.

The personal consumption expenditures price index — the Federal Reserve's preferred gauge of underlying inflation — rose 3.8% year over year for October, in line with the Dow Jones consensus estimate. Separately, economic growth came in much stronger for the third quarter. Gross domestic product rose at a 4.3% annualized rate, according to a revised Commerce Department estimate.

The data reinforced expectations for another Fed rate hike in December.

"[I]t is more urgent than ever that Fed officials step up the pace of interest rate hikes to cool off economic demand," said Chris Rupkey, chief economist at FWDBONDS. "Rate hikes are coming. Lots of them. Bet on it. The economy and inflation are literally on fire."

Wednesday's drop comes after yields steadied on Tuesday, with traders digesting softer demand for a 5-year Treasury auction.

Trading is shortened this week due to the Thanksgiving holiday. Bond markets will be closed all day Thursday for Thanksgiving, and will close at 2 p.m. E.T. on Friday.

#2780. Kalshi traders say state data center moratoriums are more likely than not

After Democrats swept Congress and key governor races during the midterm elections, Kalshi traders put the odds that at least four states would pause data center construction at about 75%. Since Nov. 22, the probability that at least four states would adopt moratoriums rose again, to 57% from 52%.

The contract asks "How many states will adopt data center moratoriums this year?" and will be verified from state governments. On July 14, New York became the first state to impose a one-year pause on certain state permits for hyperscale data centers. Various local governments have also ordered moratoriums on data centers this year.

#2779. This Anthropic proxy play is getting traders' attention – here's where they see it going

Who says you can't tailgate an IPO?Traders are preparing for this week's blockbuster Anthropic IPO in different ways, including buying options on one publicly-traded fund that invests in private assets.The Destiny Tech100 (DXYZ) is a publicly-traded, closed-end fund that invests in equity, equity-linked instruments, or shares of private funds and venture companies to gain exposure to private companies. Currently, 14% of the fund's assets are tied to Anthropic, according to the company's website.Options volume on the fund jumped to more than 10x the 30-day average Monday, according to Cboe LiveVol data. Of the 42,300 contracts traded by midday, 41,800 were calls, with just 141 puts likely opened by buyers.

Just shy of $5.5 million in options premium on DXYZ traded on Monday, with almost all of it tied to calls, but just marginally more spent buying than selling calls, according to SpotGamma. The highest-volume contract is currently the 30-strike call expiring Friday, a 90-cent contract that needs the fund to rally 19% into the weekend to pay off.Traders may want to be wary of how the fund traded around the SpaceX IPO: DXYZ surged almost 150% in May before unwinding all the gains in the weeks ahead of SpaceX's debut. The AI giant is expected to price Wednesday evening and begin trading on the Nasdaq on Thursday under the ticker ANT.

#2777. One Possession: Baylor Scheierman provides key late-game scoring for Celtics

One Possession: Baylor Scheierman provides key late-game scoring for Celtics originally appeared on NBC Sports Boston

Baylor Scheierman produced the prettiest pass of the Boston Celtics' entire four-game road trip on Tuesday night … but didn't even get the assist.

That's kind of the way things have gone lately for Boston's bench, which has been well intentioned throughout Boston's four-game trip, but simply misfired on an absurd amount of quality looks.

This time it was rookie Chris Cenac Jr. who smoked a cutting layup off Scheierman's pretty feed through traffic. But QB1 still found a way to leave his stamp on Boston's streak-busting win over the Wizards on Tuesday night.

Scheierman shook off his own rough shooting on this four-game road trip to score eight points in a 75-second span late in the fourth quarter. The Wizards had whittled Boston's 34-point lead down to five in crunch time but Scheierman, getting the late-game nod from coach Joe Mazzulla, hit a clutch 3-pointer off a feed from Jayson Tatum to put Boston up nine with 2:31 to go.

Some hustle hunting an offensive rebound on Boston's next offensive trip put Scheierman at the free throw line for two more points, then he capped his late-game outburst by knocking down a 31-foot bomb off another feed from Tatum.

Scheierman was a mere 3-of-19 shooting (15.8 percent) during Boston's four-game losing streak before finding his groove in the fourth quarter against the Wizards.

Boston desperately needs some of their young wings to differentiate themselves this season and Scheierman continues to be the steadiest of the bunch. Even as his shot defied him on this trip, Scheierman made good things happen with his basketball IQ.

That should have included the touchdown pass to Cenac Jr., with Boston's bench ready to explode after the highlight-worthy feed.

Still, as the Celtics risked letting another big lead evaporate, it was Scheierman who made some clutch late-game shots. And might have saved Thanksgiving for the Celtics.

#2771. Romeo Doubs returns to Patriots practice ahead of Chargers game

Another member of the New England Patriots starting offense has returned to practice.

Two days after left guard Alijah Vera-Tucker, wide receiver Romeo Doubs made his comeback on Wednesday. His participation at practice indicates that the Patriots have opened his 21-day window to be activated from injured reserve to the 53-man roster.

Doubs, 26, missed the last four games on injured reserve because of a shoulder ailment he suffered on a touchdown catch in Week 6 against the New York Jets.

In addition to Doubs, safety Craig Woodson also returned to practice on Wednesday.

#2770. Prediction markets push OpenAI IPO expectations later into 2027

Anthropic's shaky performance on the public markets since its debut last week and a continuing tech sell-off may be changing the outlook for when prediction markets think OpenAI will go public.

Speculators on prediction market platform Kalshi think there's a roughly 28% chance the ChatGPT developer officially announces an IPO by June 2027. That figure was most recently above 50% earlier this month.

Even by October 2027, odds are still only 50-50 that the private company — most recently conducting a funding round at a $1.25 trillion valuation — will make its move to the public markets.

Kalshi defines officially announcing an IPO as either the Securities and Exchange Commission declaring an S-1 form as effective, the IPO being priced or an exchange has assigned a ticker to the company. Contracts are resolved using reports from media outlets.

OpenAI confidentially filed for an IPO in June, but then in an interview with Fortune published in September CEO Sam Altman ruled out a 2026 public markets debut as questions over artificial intelligence safety grew.

The company's chief rival, Anthropic, debuted on the public markets last week. However, the stock has declined nearly 30% from its peak during its first trading session, and on Tuesday it closed below its IPO price of $83.

Meanwhile, a perpetual futures contract for OpenAI on Hyperliquid has tumbled more than 30% since highs in early October. In late October, OpenAI pared back its goal for its recent funding round to $20 billion from $30 billion.

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2769. NBA power rankings: How far will slumping Thunder, Celtics fall?

As the NBA calendar churns on, Thanksgiving will give fans the chance to catch their breath — and, perhaps, catch some football.

No games will be played Thursday, Nov. 26, though NBA Cup action is starting to ramp up.

Another thing ramping up are the stakes, as certain contenders are facing adversity and losing skids. Chief among them are the Oklahoma City Thunder, who have been besieged by injuries and lost six of their last eight. In the East, it's the Boston Celtics who tumbled on a four-game losing streak, though they snapped it Tuesday, Nov. 24 against the Wizards. How far will both those teams fall?

Here are the Week 5 USA TODAY Sports NBA power rankings:

Movement from previous rankings in parentheses

Tyler Herro has embraced efficiency and has been on a scoring flash. Milwaukee's first win of November (against the Raptors) was a nice boost.

With all their injuries, they've had six different players lead the team in scoring over their last six games. That said, points are still too tough to come by.

A gritty win against the Celtics in which the Kings held Boston to just 13 points in the third quarter was a glimpse of what this roster can do when playing well. The problem? That doesn't happen anywhere near enough.

The offense has moments when it struggles to make baskets, but no team allows more points per 100 possessions than Brooklyn does, a staggering 120.3.

The Clippers rank second in the NBA in 3-point percentage (38.1%) but are middle of the pack in attempts (37.8 per game; tied for 15th).

Losers of 11 of their last 12, the Hornets have serious offensive issues and rank last in scoring, averaging just 100.6 points per game.

New Orleans isn't much better and has lost 10 of 11. The Pels also rank dead last in 3-point attempts per game (30.3).

Scottie Barnes has been stellar, but this team simply won't compete as long as Kawhi Leonard is sidelined. Toronto has lost seven of eight.

The Bulls have been far better as of late, with wins over the last 10 days against three teams in the top 15 of this list: the Hawks, Timberwolves and Cavaliers.

In their last game, they beat the injured Thunder (Shai Gilgeous-Alexander did play, however). That was a positive, but the Pacers offense cannot afford to be without Tyrese Haliburton. The good news? He practiced with Indiana's G League squad.

Anthony Davis has been superb and has stayed healthy, but Washington's other offensive weapons haven't been as consistent. Still, three wins in their last four is solid for the Wizards.

Franz Wagner was keeping Orlando competitive, but it has become clear, as teams have started to scout the Magic, that they miss Paolo Banchero (ankle). Even when he returns, however, he'll need reps to figure out how he jells with Wagner in new coach Sean Sweeney's system.

Since Stephen Curry (oblique) went down, the offensive production simply hasn't been there to sustain competitive play. Golden State ranks last in points per game (94.5) over the last six games.

Even though he hasn't been out long, and even though his production has dipped this season, the Cavs have regressed on defense and miss the scoring of Evan Mobley (toe).

After a slow start in which the offense lagged, Phoenix has won eight of its last 10. The Suns are doing a nice job of sharing the ball and limiting turnovers.

The other night against the Heat, the Thunder dressed just nine players because of injury. Their 10th place standing in the West is the lowest they've been since April 9 … of 2023.

They let the Mavericks clamp them up to go on a 17-0 run Tuesday night to close out the first half and begin the second, leading to a 21-point deficit. Low effort on transition defense compounded everything.

This young team has had some solid wins this season. And, with his blend of scoring and play-making, Cooper Flagg is well on his way to his first (of many) All-Star appearances.

It's clear Deni Avdija and Damian Lillard are the offensive catalysts. But since Ja Morant (hamstring) went out, no player has emerged as a third option.

The Grizz have continued to be one of the top surprises this season, but their star rookie, No. 3 overall pick Cameron Boozer, missing a month may set them back a touch.

Very quietly, the Hawks have been building a terrific season. They're the only team to average more than 30 assists per game, they rank fourth in second-chance points per game (16.9) and they've been playing cohesive and collaborative team defense.

Live by the 3, die by it; during Boston's recent four-game losing streak, which it snapped Tuesday night against the Wizards, the Celtics shot just 28.3% from beyond the arc.

They own the NBA's longest winning streak (seven games), have incorporated more on-ball screening and pick-and-rolls and an encouraging sign was that the offense rolled against a very depleted Thunder squad while Giannis Antetokounmpo was out.

After a slow start, the defending champs have settled in. They're leading the league in rebound percentage (53.8%). Karl-Anthony Towns leads the NBA in rebounding (13.9 per game) and hasn't had a single game in which he didn't reach double figures in the stat.

One of the more positive signs for Detroit has been its defense, which ranks fourth in rating (108.3) and tied for second in blocks per game (5.8) — all without Jalen Duren.

Houston has dropped two of its last four, both against solid opponents, but the Rockets have embraced efficiency, ranking third in true shooting (59.9%) and effective field goal percentage (56.0%).

Ayo Dosunmu has been a revelation for the Timberwolves with Anthony Edwards (knee) out, and his steady scoring has kept the offense afloat.

With Autin Reaves (heel) out, things have been shakier lately, though just one loss in their last 12 shows this team has staying power. But L.A. could use a Reaves return.

Joel Embiid's illness has come at a tough time, as he had just started to find his offensive dominance, scoring 29.7 points over his last three.

Even without Dylan Harper (hip), the Spurs are thriving, and Victor Wembanyama is playing at an MVP level. San Antonio also leads the NBA in net rating (+12.3).

#2767. Nvidia has lost $2 trillion in value. Here's what options traders expect from earnings

Jensen Huang's forced exit from Nvidia shocked Wall Street and Washington, wiped $2 trillion off its market value, and raised serious questions about national security and regulation of the AI complex. Traders, for the moment, must content themselves with the comparatively mundane business of Tuesday's earnings, and options traders are expecting big moves.

With volatility in the stock near the highest since April 2025, options traders are now pricing a 7% reaction in the stock after its report, the biggest implied move going into an Nvidia report since February 2025, and well above the median move of 3.2% over the last eight quarters.

Implied volatility reached 68 Monday, compared to under 40 in early October, according to ThinkOrSwim data. Nvidia vol reached a multi-year high above 92 in the aftermath of President Trump's tariff Liberation day in April '25. In other words, the company that for much of the AI boom was the most reliable winner in the market, is now more volatile than all but 35 companies in the S&P 500.

"Longer term, I see significant downside risk unless Nvidia can attract an exceptional leader, someone like Tim Cook or AMD's Lisa Su," Nigam Arora, founder of the Arora report, said in a text ahead of the company's after-market report on Tuesday.

Since hitting all-time highs in October, Nvidia shares have fallen 35%, shedding $2 trillion in market capitalization along the way. Including Nvidia, there are only five companies in the S&P 500 worth more than $2 trillion. However, there is evidence that bulls have been holding out hope while bears snatch up profits: the ratio of open puts to call contracts has actually widened towards calls, according to Barchart data. The ratio is currently 0.87, down from a one-year high of 1.02 two weeks ago and the lowest since the first week of October.

The most popular contract by volume on Monday was the 155-strike put expiring at the bell Monday, but the most popular trade for later expiry was the 160-strike call expiring Thursday, and the second-most-common purchase was the 190-strike call expiring Friday, SpotGamma data show.

One catch is that most call contracts traded Monday looked like neutral to bearish positions opened by traders selling calls. Of the 5 million options traded on Monday, 3 million were calls, but more were likely opened by sellers than buyers. Of the $2.3 billion in options premium traded on Nvidia Monday, $1.2 billion was tied to puts, SpotGamma data show.

With bears taking profits in puts the past month, there's another potential snag on open interest. There are very few open put contracts at or below the $157 level at which the stock closed Monday. That suggests any bad surprise would find many traders with little downside protection.

By the same token, there's notable open interest in call contracts from the 175-strike and above, suggesting profit-taking on a bounce could limit Nvidia's recovery past levels it breached over the past week.

Either way, Tuesday's earnings could provide fireworks.

#2764. Jensen Huang tells staff that Nvidia doesn't need a succession plan

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang on Tuesday struck a conciliatory and remorseful tone while leading his first employee meeting since being reinstated, and said how sorry he was for what he had done.

Huang told employees that he let them down and promised to do a better job the second time around, according to people inside the room, who asked not to be named in order to share details. "I hurt all of you," Huang said, according to one of the people.

The CEO also addressed succession planning during the event, the people said. He told staff that Nvidia has built a team that doesn't require succession planning the way a normal organization does, because the company operates a unique business model where there are multiple capable leaders at Nvidia that are leading different parts of the organization.

A succession plan would create unnecessary hierarchy, Huang said, according to one of the people present.

Investors and analysts were left to speculate about the company's future leadership in the wake of Huang's resignation on Nov. 12.

On Monday, Huang was reinstated as CEO of the chipmaker by Nvidia's board of directors. Eleven days before, Huang had resigned from Nvidia after multiple reported affairs, including one with a Chinese businesswoman who is alleged to be a spy for Beijing.

Harvey Jones, Nvidia's newly appointed board chair, said last week that Huang resigned after "disclosure of personal behavior inconsistent with the company's code of conduct, standards and cultural norms."

Huang started the meeting by thanking CFO Colette Kress for her leadership as interim CEO.

Huang told staff that Tuesday's company meeting was the second hardest meeting of his life, the first one being with his wife. He said he is in therapy, according to one of the people present.

In a press release announcing Huang's return on Monday, the company said the board "received input" from senior officials in the federal government about any potential national security risk around the CEO. President Donald Trump told CNBC that he gave the board assurances and "made the deal" to restore Huang to his post.

After the company had its worst week in the stock market since 2008, the stock surged more than 30% the day his return was announced. Shares fell on Tuesday, however.

An Nvidia representative didn't immediately respond to requests for comment.

Nov. 25—WILSON CREEK — The Wilson Creek Devils have been hard at work this offseason. Devils Head Coach Jeff Sackmann said things have continued moving in the right direction as the girls began their preseason practices.

"I'm excited; the girls are excited," said Sackmann. "I kept hearing all fall how excited the girls were for basketball season, and they definitely have been enthusiastic, and we've had a great effort and good turnout."

Wilson Creek is set to return its full roster from last season and will have senior leadership from Lila Sackmann and Riley Larsen.

"They understand that this is their last year to play high school sports," said Sackmann. "I saw it at the end of the volleyball season; the seniors really stepped up and played some of the best they played all year in the last few games and I think that attitude carries over."

The effort and enthusiasm shown by these players has become a point of emphasis on the team as they have begun displaying better communication on the court and have been very encouraging of one another.

"They've really been good in that regard, and I hope we can keep it going," Sackmann said.

Also acting as a leader on the team this year is junior Kaydence Leslie.

"She's doing a great job as well," said the coach. "She's stepping up more as far as being more of a local leader than she was last year."

Early season practices have seen 13 girls on the court, which Sackmann said has been a major increase in attendance since he started with only eight.

Last year, the Devils had a solid run through the postseason, dominating Easton in the opening round before upsetting the Soap Lake Eagles the next day. While they fell into consolation against Waterville-Mansfield, they went on to have a chance at state after beating Entiat.

Wilson Creek got all the way into the District 5 third-place game to qualify for state before falling to the Moses Lake Christian Academy Lions to end their season. The coach said this loss has fueled the team as they've worked toward the basketball season again.

"We definitely felt some momentum (last season) when we got to the playoffs," said the coach. "We won a game where we were the lower seed and then we were kind of playing with house money after that and really finished the season strong."

While the girls have a talented roster of both upperclassmen and improving young players, they are still lacking in the height department, he said. To combat this, practices have been focused on controlling the pace of the game and playing fast.

"They're hungry and motivated," said Sackmann. "We had a really good summer, and a few girls put some extra work in just on their own this summer and this fall. They're ready to go."

With all the work both he and the athletes put in during the offseason, the coach said he was just as excited as his players to get back into the basketball season.

"I put in a lot of time over the summer working on some new things that we wanted to try, so it was really exciting to get into the gym and finally get to try some of the stuff out," said Sackmann. "(We're excited) to put it to the test when we finally do get to live games and play against some teams that we've either lost to or had close games with last year and see how we measure up, see how much we can improve over last year."

#2760. Ukraine tells Putin to ‘stop complaining’ over oil refinery attacks as Russia promises retaliation

Ukrainian Foreign Minister Andrii Sybiha has told Russian President Vladimir Putin to "stop complaining" over attacks on Russian refineries, saying Putin could end Kyiv's strikes on the oil facilities by bringing an end to the Kremlin's nearly five-year-long war.

His comments come shortly after Russia's president told a meeting of government officials and senior oil executives in the Kremlin on Tuesday that Ukraine continues to attack refining facilities despite U.S. President Donald Trump's appeal to refrain from doing so.

"I would like to note that retaliatory action from our side is inevitable. Everyone must be aware of that," Putin said, according to Russian state news agency Tass.

In September, Trump called on Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy to stop targeting Russian oil refineries, saying the attacks were "hurting the world" as fuel supply disruptions from the wars in Ukraine and Iran propelled U.S. diesel prices to a record high. Moscow welcomed Trump's push for Ukraine to halt strikes on Russia's diesel-producing facilities at the time.

"If Putin doesn't like Ukraine's strikes on his oil refineries, he can order an end to the war," Ukraine's Sybiha said Tuesday via social media.

"If he doesn't want his military vessels hit, he can order an end to the war. Not to mention the 432,000 Russian soldiers he has lost on the battlefield this year without coming any closer to the goals of his invasion. Clearly, he doesn't care about them. Stop complaining. End the war," he added. CNBC could not independently verify the figures.

In July, the Center for Strategic and International Studies estimated that about 1.4 million Russian troops were killed, wounded or went missing between the start of the full-scale invasion in Feb. 2022 and June 2026, with more than 30,000 casualties a month in the first half of this year.

Ukraine has stepped up attacks on Russian critical targets in recent weeks, damaging Putin's prized Crimea bridge, striking a Russian cargo ship in the Baltic Sea and destroying one of Russia's newest ships at a Libyan port.

Kyiv has also targeted Russian energy infrastructure. Zelenskyy said earlier this month that Ukraine's long-range strikes had disabled more than 55% of Russia's oil-refining capacity, describing this as a "justified response to Russia's refusal to de-escalate and its strikes on our energy infrastructure."

Ukrainian drones reportedly struck two major Russian oil refineries in Russia's Samara region overnight, triggering fires at both facilities.

Satellite images published in late October also showed black smoke rising from multiple fires at Rosneft's Novokuibyshevsk oil refinery in southern Russia following a suspected Ukrainian drone attack.

Putin on Tuesday said Russia's fuel market situation remains "challenging" and called on government officials and oil executives "to continue reacting promptly to the supply-demand relationship for fuel across the whole territory of Russia," according to Interfax.

Ukraine's allies have warned the country faces another winter of blackouts and freezing temperatures amid escalating Russian energy strikes.

#2759. AMD dives on short seller report of alleged China exposure

CNBC did not independently verify this information.

Hunterbrook also said that new export rules in Washington could "tighten restrictions on AMD's foreign business," alleging that AMD "bills over 20% of its revenue to customers in China and Hong Kong," and "depends on a Chinese partner to finish most of its chips."

The allegations come amid investigations of rival chipmaker Nvidia over its sales to companies in Asia with ties to China. AMD has been viewed as a beneficiary of the uncertainty surrounding Nvidia.

"The claims misrepresent AMD's business practices and relationships," an AMD spokesperson said in a statement. "AMD complies with applicable US export control laws and regulations and maintains rigorous processes to ensure compliance across our global operations. We remain focused on serving our customers and upholding the highest standards of integrity."

Lisa Su, CEO of AMD, told investors on the company's recent earnings call that China is an "important" market and that the semiconductor giant wants to be fully compliant with U.S. export controls.

AMD continued to slide after hours, trading down 0.45% to $487 shortly after 4 p.m.

#2757. Investors led by U.S. billionaire Todd Boehly to buy Lukoil's oil and gas assets outside Russia, sources say

Investors led by U.S. billionaire Todd Boehly's asset management company Eldridge have struck a deal to buy Lukoil's assets outside of Russia, sources confirmed to CNBC.

The U.S. International Development Finance Corporation and Catena Investments, based in the United Arab Emirates, are also parties to the deal, the sources said.

The deal transfers Lukoil's international refineries and other production assets in Bulgaria, Romania, Africa and the Middle East to the U.S.-led group, the sources said. It also includes retail gas stations in the U.S., Europe and Asia, they said.

The U.S. Office of Foreign Assets Control is expected to sign off on the deal but final approval is still needed, the sources said. The deal would give the U.S.-led group about 350,000 barrels per day of production.

The agreement would allow Europe to buy fuel such as diesel previously produced by Lukoil through the U.S.-led group rather than Russia.

#2755. Rookie Yaxel Lendeborg's maturation with Steph Curry out evaluated by Steve Kerr

Rookie Yaxel Lendeborg's maturation with Steph Curry out evaluated by Steve Kerr originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

The Warriors are in the midst of a rough stretch.

Golden State started the 2026-27 NBA season at 0-3 before going on an eight-game winning streak. But star guard Steph Curry suffered an injury in a Nov. 11 game against the Houston Rockets, and the team lost five straight before beating the Boston Celtics on Sunday at Chase Center.

#2754. Liesman: Nvidia whiplash tests Kevin Warsh’s push to read market signals

The extreme market gyrations and the drama surrounding Nvidia this past week have highlighted the dilemma facing Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh.

On Friday, as stocks sold off, market-based probabilities that the Fed would raise interest rates declined. On Monday, with Jensen Huang back in office, a powerful rally restored market confidence that two more rate hikes are coming.

On one level, ignoring daily market vicissitudes and focusing on the long term is at the heart of one of the key changes Warsh wants to push through at the Fed. On another, he has been explicit that markets do a better job of forecasting and he wants to use market indicators more than forecasts to guide policy.

But those indicators can be subject to extreme volatility.

Take the benchmark Goldman Sachs Financial Conditions Index. Last week, the market decline and widening of credit spreads had sent a signal for the Fed to ease back on at least some of its plans to tighten policy. Some Fed commentators called for rate hikes to be taken off the table entirely. On Monday, the index eased by 22 basis points, reversing nearly half of the prior week's tightening, and both December and January hikes were back on the table.

Warsh's message, in words and deeds, is for markets to look beyond that volatility. He traveled to San Francisco last week and patiently presided for three hours over a conference on the outlook for artificial intelligence and its potential economic impact, peppering economists and tech leaders with questions about the dangers and potential upside of AI for the economy. The meeting was part of the AI task force he has convened that, in turn, is one of five to study different areas of Fed policymaking.

In his opening remarks, he said, "These groups are a way of broadening our thinking, of inviting robust discussions that take us far deeper... than fleeting data points [or] daily market moves. Our task forces are an intellectual investment to help better equip us for the next policy conjuncture and beyond."

Compare that desire for intellectual investment to Warsh's press conferences after Fed meetings. He has cut them down to a half hour, and he routinely spends much of the time parrying reporter questions about what the next Fed move will be, part of his general aversion to forward guidance. At the June press conference, he paraphrased former Secretary of State George Schultz saying, "Press conferences are useful. But when you have one, you want to make sure you have something important to say." In a regime where Warsh does not want to provide forward guidance, there is far less to say than there once was.

Here's what is known of what Warsh seems to believe about monetary policy:

In a sense, it seems, Warsh wants to make monetary policy the way he did his old job at the Duquesne Family office working for legendary investor Stan Druckenmiller. There, he evaluated startup companies, many of them with the wisp of an idea in the mind of a tech geek, for the possibility of profit many years down the road. Marked to market, those investments would show big negative numbers on the day's profit and loss statement. Down the road, some would go bankrupt and others would offer handsome returns.

Warsh's focus on AI highlights his dilemma in wanting to do policy in a different, more forward-looking and market-based way. The outlook for AI, as the chairman himself has admitted, is moving rapidly and is deeply uncertain. Markets may already be rethinking the potential earnings upside and the amount that will be spent on the AI buildout. The adoption rate of the technology is uncertain, and so are the economic effects on inflation and growth.

Warsh in San Francisco showed a particular interest in the potential for a "China Shock" to hit jobs in the U.S. service sector, a reference to how Chinese imports devastated the U.S. manufacturing sector earlier this century. If that happened, widespread unemployment could lead the Fed to lower rates over time, while the frenzied AI buildout looks now to be sparking inflation and pressuring rates upward. So there are different messages for the Fed chairman being sent from the same economic development.

It is established in the monetary policy literature that the current rate level affects the economy with what are known as "long and variable lags." Those lags mean that where policy is set today will influence the economy sometime next year. The objective is to set policy for where one thinks the economy will be next year.

Wide error bands surround most economic forecasts, including those from the Fed. But there are similarly wide error bands on market pricing. The question is, which market signals are worth watching, and for how long do they need to persist to guide policy? The Nvidia situation shows markets can be very short-term about the long-term signals they send.

#2753. Anthropic was supposed to save the AI trade. What went wrong?

Anthropic's historic IPO — the largest in U.S. history — delivered a big win for the company and its investors. But it failed at another task: reviving animal spirits in the AI trade.

The Nasdaq Composite closed down 2.25% amid a broader market pullback with the S&P 500 declining 2% to 7,175.

Investors had been looking forward to the Anthropic IPO as a "clearing event" — reviving the tech sector after months of choppy trading, the Nvidia espionage scandal, and growing concerns that AI chip sales to Asia are likely to slump as Washington cracks down on potential violations of export controls.

Some of those worries resurfaced Thursday as a short seller, Hunterbrook Media, alleged in a report that firmware sold by chipmaker AMD had turned up in U.S.-blacklisted companies. AMD stock closed down more than 7%. The chipmaker told CNBC it complies with U.S. law.

Adding to the worries, the House Foreign Affairs Committee released documents showing that a subagency of the Commerce Department approved the sale of more than 879,000 AI chips to Chinese companies. That also raised the prospect of an Asia sales slowdown under stricter export controls.

Many investors and analysts continue to express confidence in the AI trade, noting that Anthropic's successful market debut — the stock closed up 17% from its IPO price and raised more than $81 billion — bodes well for capital spending across tech.

But investors acknowledged that this is a high-tension moment and said it's time to be more strategic about the trade.

Dan Niles, founder of Niles Investment Management, described himself as a "circumspect bull," making the comparison to market conditions in 2022 when the big indices tanked in the back half of the year.

"You could have still made money that year. You just needed to have the right combination of longs and shorts," he said.

Part of the challenge, Niles said, has been a price war between the two cutting-edge frontier labs, Anthropic and OpenAI. That has posed a threat to the overall revenue and profit potential of AI and the capex going into it. Fierce competition on price is producing collective losses between the two top companies, he notes.

"The combined revenue growth for OpenAI plus Anthropic went from growing 75% sequentially in Q2 to 48% in Q3," he told CNBC. "The combined losses went from $7.9 billion to about $8.4 billion from Q2 to Q3."

While uncertainty is high now, some resolution may be on the horizon if a greater degree of commercial and regulatory clarity emerges over the next few months.

Trading experts also told CNBC that the tech sector's troubles Thursday were a more serious matter than Anthropic simply siphoning capital from other parts of the market.

"There's a reset of AI in general," said JJ Kinahan, senior vice president at Cboe Global Markets.

Investors for years have profited in tech by buying the dip, he said, but "there's been an attitude change that [you] can't buy things blindly and they'll all go up." Nvidia's ongoing declines have shaken confidence in the buy-the-dip strategy, he said.

Anthropic's next few trading days will be a key gauge of the market's strength. Friday will be a "wild card," Kinahan said. It could be a "final flush out" for the market, or a bounce that leaves the market in "no man's land" while volatility remains elevated.

A better gauge may be Monday, when options on Anthropic start trading, providing insight into whether retail investors will back the company in force — providing more of a vote of confidence in the AI trade.

"If people think we're going up [on Anthropic] they'll buy stock, but retail particularly will just buy calls," he said. "There's a bigger test coming Monday."

For now, investors continue to sell AI-related stocks.

Neoclouds, the generally highly indebted AI-dedicated computing providers at the outer edges of the AI trade, were down substantially Thursday, with CoreWeave closing down more than 6%.

Hyperscalers such as Alphabet and Amazon.com, viewed as relatively defensive AI bets, also finished lower.

One bright spot: Cybersecurity stocks continued to rally, with Palo Alto Networks and CrowdStrike both closing up. AI is finding security holes across software infrastructure, creating a scramble to patch the vulnerabilities before hackers can exploit them.

— CNBC's Daren Fonda contributed reporting.

#2752. Furious second half rally falls short, and Wizards lose to Celtics

At halftime, I got started on an article about the Wizards based on a question from a friend. Because who wanted to read about the Wizards getting demolished? Again. I didn't want to write about another blowout. But, I decided to give it a few minutes into the second half before focusing on the other topic, and voila — the Wizards rallied.

The first half was putrid. The Wizards' offensive rating (points per possession x 100) was a woeful 93. Their defensive rating was a disgraceful 154. The Celtics hit 15-26 from three, good for 57.7%. Washington trailed, 80-49. Those 80 points were the most Boston has scored in a half this season. Folks, when the Celtics lost to the Golden State Warriors on Sunday, they scored 87 points total. This was a butt-kicking.

In the second half, the script flipped. Washington's offnesive rating was a superb 137. Their defensive rating was an excellent 104. And they whittled Boston's lead to as little as five with just over three minutes to play.

Alas, they couldn't close the deal, Boston hit some shots late, and the final margin ballooned back to 15.

Now, no one should get too chesty about the second half defense. Washington's overall effort seemed stronger, but Boston still got open looks. A lot of the better result was a matter of Boston missing shots they hit in the first — 7-25 from three in the third and fourth quarters vs. 15-26 in the first two.

The bright side is that Washington played hard in the second half after getting paddled in the first. This is a franchise that has a long tradition of giving up when the going gets tough, and last night they didn't.

I'm still not sure why Reggie Miller persists as a lead analyst in these games. He's a likable character, and he sometimes makes good points about the game. He also says things that have…well…let's call it a tenuous connection with reality. Last night, for example, he talked about how the Wizards should be better than they are (debatable, but that's his opinion, so whatever) and went on to say, "…they should be competing for the play-in…" uttered in a tone that suggested they weren't. Except, the Wizards entered the night ninth in the Eastern Conference, which means THEY'D BE IN THE PLAY-IN.

The final possession of the first quarter is an illustration of why it's so tough to give up on Trae Young. He drove against stout pressure from Boston's Hugo Gonzalez, seemed to be going up for a floater, before spotting a cutting Kyshawn George, whereupon he looked a defender out of position and then tossed a perfect lob for a George dunk.

Interesting to see Anthony Davis play the entire first quarter. Unfortunately, when he went to the bench, the game spiraled.

About the nine-minute mark of the second quarter, there as a defensive possession sure to get some time at the team's next film session. A whole mess of Wizards defenders got beat on a Paul George drive. Bilal Coulibaly spotted it from the weakside, teleported across the lane to help, and forced a George kickout pass to the weakside. Coulibaly looked back at his man (Peyton Pritchard) and was clearly startled to see that none of his teammates was even attempting a rotation. Coulibaly sprinted into a closeout, but Pritchard nailed the wide-open three.

On that same play, Deandre Ayton got eliminated from the play by a Derrick White screen. I can't remember the last time I saw a seven-footer show so little fight when getting screened. By no coincidence, Ayton did not make a second half appearance.

Another second quarter defensive mishap — Ayton helped at the three-point line for reasons that were not entirely clear. For reasons that were even less clear, AJ Dybantsa sprinted up from the low block, cut between Ayton and a Celtics player, and contested a three-point attempt, which missed. That left the lane open for Luka Garza to stroll down the lane for the putback. Just to be clear, Ayton should not have been helping in that spot — at most, he should have hedged and recovered to Garza or the paint. Dybantsa should not have been closing out — he should have either stayed put on the block or rotated onto Garza.

2:07 of the second quarter — Dybantsa had a highlight reel dunk. This kid!

At about the two-minute mark, more infuriating defense. On this play, Tatum got a dunk off a basic backscreen that got him a step on Dybantsa. The problem: Young was unaware his man was screening, didn't call out the screen, and then didn't pick up Tatum. The result: Tatum jogged into a dunk.

In the third quarter, there was more Young defensive cluelessness. Young's man set an off-ball brush screen on George's man. Young didn't notice his man was screening, didn't call it out, and didn't switch. Another easy at-rim score. George looked bad, but that was on Young.

At 8:41 of the third quarter, the Wizards tried zone. Sam Hauser walked up to the top of the circle from inside the paint. Davis had to follow because Hauser is a shooting threat. Meanwhile, Boston ran a pick-and-roll action up top, Neemias Queta rolled hard, which got him open because Dybantsa hesitated on the switch. Add in George rotating late from the strongside corner, and it was an easy at-rim finish for Queta.

Late in the third quarter, Boston followed the NBA-wide plan and conceded three-point attempts to Coulibaly. Last night, Coulibaly hit two in a row. It'd be nice if that somehow continued.

Earlier I gave an example of why it's tough to give up on Young. Around the 10-minute mark of the fourth quarter, Young gave an example of why you might want to. It was a terrible possession that consisted mostly of Young dribblimg around making moves, completely unable to shake Baylor Scheierman. He finally jacked up a long stepback two, which he missed.

With about 2:29 to play, Tatum was working on Khris Middleton, who was holding up in the post. Young stood in no-man's land for a few seconds before weakly jumping in to double for no discernible reason — Tatum was moving away from the basket, Middleton was in good position, and the shot clock was running down. That left Scheierman wide open at the three-point line. Good shooter that he is, he hit the shot. This was an example of needlessly helping from one pass away.

One last defensive blunder — with 1:16 remaining, Scheierman got another wide open three, which he made. Young was on him to start the possession. He seemed to think he was switching…onto Tatum…when Scheierman set a ghost screen. Dybantsa, who was on Tatum, seemed unaware of a switch (perhaps because there was no communication), and Scheierman got left alone.

Below are the four factors that decide wins and losses in basketball — shooting (efg), rebounding (offensive rebounds), ball handling (turnovers), fouling (free throws made).

eFG% (effective field goal percentage, which accounts for the three-point shot)

TOV% (turnover percentage — turnovers divided by possessions)

FTM/FGA (free throws made divided by field goal attempts)

PPA is my overall production metric, which credits players for things they do that help a team win (scoring, rebounding, playmaking, defending) and dings them for things that hurt (missed shots, turnovers, bad defense, fouls).

PPA is a per possession metric designed for larger data sets. In small sample sizes, the numbers can get weird. In PPA, 100 is average, higher is better and replacement level is 45. For a single game, replacement level isn't much use, and I reiterate the caution about small samples sometimes producing weird results.

POSS is the number of possessions each player was on the floor in this game.

ORTG = offensive rating, which is points produced per individual possessions x 100. League average so far this season is listed in the Four Factors table above. Points produced is not the same as points scored. It includes the value of assists and offensive rebounds, as well as sharing credit when receiving an assist.

USG = offensive usage rate. Average is 20%. Median last season was 17.7%.

ORTG and USG are versions of stats created by former Wizards assistant coach Dean Oliver and modified by me. ORTG is an efficiency measure that accounts for the value of shooting, offensive rebounds, assists and turnovers. USG includes shooting from the floor and free throw line, offensive rebounds, assists and turnovers.

+PTS = "Plus Points" is a measure of the points gained or lost by each player based on their efficiency in this game compared to league average efficiency on the same number of possessions. A player with an offensive rating (points produced per possession x 100) of 100 who uses 20 possessions would produce 20 points. If the league average efficiency is 115, the league — on average — would produced 23.0 points in the same 20 possessions. So, the player in this hypothetical would have a +PTS score of -3.0.

Players are sorted by total production in the game.

#2748. Trump’s meddling in Huang scandal reignites voter angst over tech’s outsized political power

When voters headed to the polls and flipped Congress this month, they sent a clear message to Washington: They wanted change.

Voters wanted curbs on the technology industry's outsized power in Washington and CEOs cozying up to President Donald Trump to sway politics in their favor.

The Monday reinstatement of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang at Trump's urging may be exactly the opposite of what voters voiced with their ballots.

"This whole ordeal only reinforces what Americans already believe about a broken political system that stands up for Big Tech CEOs and not the American people," Sacha Haworth, founder and CEO of The Tech Oversight Project, told CNBC.

Republicans are warning that Trump's unwavering support for the embattled artificial intelligence chip kingpin could further alienate voters.

"The data were so clear in the election that people don't trust big tech. The corruption message, I think, resonated a lot for Democrats in the election, and I think this sort of thing isn't helpful to Republicans in Congress who are going to be running again in two years," said Brendan Steinhauser, a GOP strategist and CEO of The Alliance for Secure AI Action, who had warned that AI data centers could be a "sleeper issue" in the lead-up to the 2026 midterms.

Before and after Election Day, voters were clear about their concern, which centered on data centers.

A Pew Research Center survey published in October found that 44% of voters considered AI very important to their vote. An NBC News exit poll found that 56% of voters said AI and data centers were crucial or a major factor in their vote for Congress. And in states including Texas, data center opposition forced GOP lawmakers who once welcomed development to change their positions.

There's a lot for tech CEOs to cheer under Trump, who was welcomed with open arms following a period of tough enforcement under President Joe Biden, and many reasons to align with the administration, said Sarah Kreps, a political scientist and professor at Cornell University who studies the intersection of technology and national security.

Chief among the reasons for celebration are the market's AI-driven gains since Trump's 2025 inauguration and his light-touch approach to tech regulation. The Nasdaq has gained nearly 21% from Jan. 17, 2025, the last trading day before the inauguration, and Tuesday's close.

"In the end, this looks like a calculated risk that the people and interests Trump prioritizes benefit more from a stable, rising market than the administration loses from the optics," Kreps said of Huang's reinstatement, adding that the Republican Party will be left to "bear the consequences" in 2028.

Outside the rarefied air of Silicon Valley and the White House, there is scant support for Huang's comeback after a series of extramarital affairs, including one with a suspected Chinese spy that critics say could have jeopardized national security and undermined the U.S.'s advantage in the AI race.

Trump sought to allay those fears this week when he told CNBC that he assured the board that Huang posed no national security threat. Instead, Trump used concerns over the CEO giving his talents to China as a major rationale behind the reinstatement push.

"The bigger threat would be losing the brain power of somebody like Jensen," Trump told CNBC this week. "That would be a threat to me. That would be a far bigger threat than anything we're talking about."

Haworth said the reinstatement raises concerns over how Huang's access to Trump benefits the company's business interests, especially as the U.S. investigates chip smuggling into China.

At the same time, Secretary of Commerce Howard Lutnick's perceived slowness to clamp down on illegal advanced AI chip sales to China, as well as his family's financial ties to Nvidia via Cantor Fitzgerald, the financial firm his sons now run, have drawn further congressional scrutiny.

"Voters are struggling with the high cost of living. They're angry about the corruption they see and a system that has been working more and more for the wealthy and well-connected, and making it harder and harder on working families," Rep. Suzan DelBene, D-Wash., chair of the Democratic Congressional Campaign Committee told CNBC.

"Corruption is an affordability issue. When they see money and favors going in places that don't help support working families, that's an affordability issue for them," DelBene said.

As Republicans try to rebuild after being routed in the midterms, the kind of leniency Trump has shown to Huang could backfire, GOP strategists told CNBC.

"Voters are tired of 'rules for thee, but not for me.' Most Americans have to live with the consequences of their decisions," said Brittany Martinez, executive director of Principles First, a center-right grassroots advocacy organization. "If you're a billionaire with the president's ear, apparently the White House will help you get your job back. That's exactly the kind of double standard that makes people wonder whose side this administration is on."

Marc Short, former chief of staff to Vice President Mike Pence and chair of Advancing American Freedom, the conservative think tank Pence founded, told CNBC that the Trump administration's involvement in Huang's reinstatement "sends a very terrible signal."

"I think it's consistent with the administration taking ownership in 41 separate private companies, to now feel that they can determine to the board who should be the CEO of one of America's biggest companies," he said.

#2747. Open Thread: A second generation of NBA players has taken over the league

Today's NBA is loaded with talent. Children today grow up competitive in the sport from a young age. For many, the familiarity begins at birth. Stephen Curry and Klay Thompson, two of the best shooters to play the game, both grew up on the hardwood. Curry's father Dell played sixteen seasons in the NBA. In 1994, he was the league's Sixth Man. Mychel Thompson, Klay's dad, was the #1 draft pick in 1978. He played thirteen seasons and picked up two rings with the Showtime-era Lakers.

Today, the Spurs have Dylan Harper, son of five-time NBA star Ron Harper. He was the second overall pick in 2025. Cameron Boozer, this year's third overall draft pick, is the son of 2-time NBA All-Star Carlos Boozer. But the ties of current players whose fathers played in the NBA is a much deeper well.

Current NBA players including Jalen Brunson, Andrew Wiggins, Domantas Sabonis, Devin Booker, Jaren Jackson, Jr., Al Horford, Jerami Grant, Luke Kornet, Gary Payton II, Tim Hardaway, Jr., Scotty Pippen, Jr., Jabari Smith, Jr., Gary Trent, Jr., Ron Harper, Jr., Trayce Jackson-Davis, Cameron Carr, Devin Carter, Reed Sheppard, Nic Claxton, Larry Nance, Jr. and his brother Pete, Jase Richardson, Jabari Walker, Darius Garland, and Bronny James all have fathers who played (or in Bronny's case still play) in the NBA.

And if you look at a younger generation, you see the trend is most likely to comnintue:

With so many of today's NBA stars growing up around the game through their fathers, it's a good time to ask — could the children of the San Antonio Spurs classic Big 3 line-up join forces someday in the NBA?

Tim Duncan's son Draven is nineteen and currently playing for Pomona-Pitzer. If that name sounds familiar, Gregg Popovich served as the head coach of their basketball program from 1979-1987. And a former basketball player by the name of Mike Budenholzer also belongs to the alma mater's Hall of Fame.

Recently, Manu Ginobili's twins Dante and Nico, age sixteen, were seen shooting hoops for their high school:

The duo, who currently play for Saint Mary's Hall, are in their junior year. Depending on their talent, they could be NBA prospects in a couple of years.

Finally, Tony Parker's sons Josh and Liam are age 12 and 10 respectively. While neither have shown an interest in basketball (and Parker does his best to keep them out of the public eye), one has to believe that the apples don't fall far from the tree. A player as talented as Parker has to have passed on some of those basketball jeans. But then again, not every offspring gets their father's talent for the game…just ask Michael Jordan.

But, just for fun… imagine a Spurs lineup ten years from now featuring the next generation of Duncan, Ginobilis, and Parkers. A San Antonio Spurs being elevated by the sons of the three stars who helped bring four championships together. I realize that even if all five kids grew up with the skills to be in the NBA, it would be a long shot to get even three of them on one team. After all, there are twenty-nine other teams who'd forbid that from ever happenin….again.

#2745. 2026 Week 12 rooting guide for Cowboys fans

Thanksgiving week is always a big one for the Cowboys and the NFL as a whole. Beyond any special halftime shows or other extra production, it's also generally when the regular season heats up as playoff scenarios begin to take clearer form.

Despite all of their tribulations so far this year, the Cowboys are still just a couple of games away from both a division title and the NFC's last wild card spot. Let's check out the current standings, and then see how the entire Week 12 slate could help or hurt Dallas.

1. San Francisco 49ers 9-1 (6-1 vs NFC)2. Detroit Lions 8-2 (5-1)3. New York Giants 6-3-1 (3-3-1)4. Carolina Panthers 5-5 (4-3)5. Chicago Bears 8-2 (6-2)6. Minnesota Vikings 7-3 (5-2)7. Green Bay Packers 6-4 (4-4)_______________________________8. Seattle Seahawks 6-4 (2-3)9. Los Angeles Rams 6-4 (4-2)10. Dallas Cowboys 5-6 (2-5)

With the Packers, Rams, and Seahawks all on a bye last week, Dallas was able to inch closer in the wild card race. It's hard to see the Cowboys leapfrogging all three of those teams into the seventh seed, but at least it's still on the table.

Even winning the NFC East isn't solely up to the Cowboys at this point. They'll need help for either lane to get into the playoffs.

How might some help come from the Week 12 schedule?

The Jaguars let us down last week, allowing New York to pull off yet another surprise win. We may have to stop calling them upsets soon, as John Harbaugh has the Giants consistently performing above expectations.

This week, the G-Men are anything but underdogs. The Colts appear to be in full tank mode now, and even a 3-3 home record doesn't give much room for confidence. If anything, we have to hope that New York is the team that's feeling itself and allows Indy to catch them by surprise.

You can go either way on Washington at this point. On the one hand, you'd like them to keep winning and hurt their draft position. On the other, you don't want them suddenly having something to play for when they meet Dallas in Week 18.

A loss to Arizona would certainly take the wind out of their sails, and keep the NFC East feeling like a two-horse race. That feels like the better option this week, but it's truly a 50/50 proposition.

Los Angeles winning the Wednesday night game is doubly good for Dallas. It pulls Green Bay down a peg, who is currently our biggest concern of those 6-4 teams with a head-to-head advantage. It also helps the value of the 2027 first-round pick they owe us. If someone's going to get that seventh seed aside from the Cowboys, we definitely don't want it to be Green Bay.

Speaking of NFC North teams, the best thing for Dallas is that the Bears, Lions, and Vikings all stay jumbled at the top and lock the Packers out of a possible division win. The Packers have division games left against the Bears and Lions, and we want to see their rivals motivated in those contests.

The NFC West is a different story, with the Niners looking ready to run right on through the Super Bowl. They can have the division, at least, and help the Cowboys by putting losses on our wild card competition like the Seahawks and Rams along the way.

In the one division that makes the NFC East look competent this year, the NFC South "winner" is guaranteed to be at least the fourth seed in the playoffs. If the Cowboys do slide into the tournament as NFC East champions, they'd rather be the third seed if possible. So whoever wins the South, may they have the worst record possible.

This week's only interconference game is a real nothing burger for Cowboys fans. The best thing we can say about a Saints loss is that it pushes them further up the draft order and helps block the Eagles. So there's that.

For purely draft-related reasons: Dallas is owed the Steelers' 4th-round pick and the Patriots' 7th-rounder in 2027. So the worse their records are, the better.

#2744. Cathie Wood’s Ark Invest was a big buyer of Anthropic as she tells CNBC she’s ‘quite optimistic’

Cathie Wood was a major buyer of Anthropic following its debut Thursday on the Nasdaq, adding shares in all her major funds.

Wood's firm, which already owned private shares of the artificial intelligence company in its Ark Venture Fund leading up to the initial public offering, bought $90.8 million worth of Anthropic in six funds, according to a trade notification from the firm.

"We've owned Anthropic since early '23," Wood told CNBC's "Squawk Box" on Friday. "It's our best performing equity by far in ARKVX, our venture fund. ... Our confidence has grown with time as it really took over the enterprise" AI business.

Anthropic shares jumped 17% in their first day, but closed well off the highs of the session and were headed for sizable losses Friday. The Nasdaq, which was expected to get a boost from a major IPO reviving enthusiasm about the technology trade, fell by 2% on Thursday.

"We're climbing a wall of worry," said Wood. "Everybody is worried about AI hype and the political situation after the election, but I think we'll look back and say, OK this is more like the '80s and '90s, climbing a wall of worry you know with bricks much like what we have today: inflation, interest rates, wars, new technologies and so forth so quite optimistic here."

The Anthropic stakes are relatively small across the funds, but Wood typically will buy shares in companies she likes steadily during long stretches. Wood said she is "averaging down" with her purchases of Anthropic.

In her $6.1 billion Ark Innovation ETF, Anthropic is a 0.3% stake. The biggest holdings in that fund are Tesla and SpaceX, representing more than 21% of the fund together.

Ark Innovation, which retail investors piled into during the pandemic as it gained about 150% in 2020, tumbled the subsequent next two years, losing 67% in 2022 alone. Wood's following was dented after the big losses.

The fund had a comeback stretch as the bull market revved back up from 2023 to 2025, but has fallen on hard times again this year, down more than 10% in 2026. The S&P 500 is up 4.8% this year.

Wood dismissed comparisons of today's environment to the dot-com bubble.

"Back then if an Anthropic had gone public, it probably would have gone up threefold the first day," she said. "That's how crazy things were."

#2743. NFL Predictions: Bears will get their first win in Detroit since 2021

Thursday is the biggest regular-season game between the Chicago Bears and the Detroit Lions in a really long time. The Athletic's Kevin Fishbain dug up that the only two years both the Bears and Lions made the playoffs were 1994 and 1991. With each team currently holding a 90% chance to make the postseason, the odds are pretty good that it happens again.

In 1994, both teams snuck into the playoffs at 9-7, but in 1991 the Lions won the NFC Central at 12-4, with the Bears right behind at 11-5. They split the series in '91, with the Bears winning at Soldier Field and the Lions winning ugly at the Pontiac Silverdome on Thanksgiving, 16-6.

The winner of this Thanksgiving Day game will move to 9-2, be the two-seed in the NFC, and claim the top spot in the division with just six games to go.

Our partners at FanDuel Sportsbook have the Bears as a 1.5-point favorite (it opened at 2.5, so money has been coming in on the Lions) with the over/under at 55.5, which is the highest point total this week. I went back and forth on my prediction all week, and I can see it going either way, but give me the Bears to win, 28 to 27.

So that's the Bears and the moneyline (-122), the Lions and the 1.5 points (-112), and the under (-112).

Not only is Ben Johnson looking for his first win over the Lions, but Caleb Williams is too. The last time the Bears beat Detroit, Justin Fields had 289 combined yards, Chicago sacked Jared Goff 4 times, and he had 3 turnovers.

The last time the Bears won in Detroit, Andy Dalton threw for 317 yards, with Darnell Mooney catching 5 for 123.

Did you realize the Lions are just 1-8 on Thanksgiving since 2017? The Bears are 3-1 against them on Turkey Day in that stretch, with the only loss coming in 2024 when Matt Eberflus was a deer in headlights in the waning seconds, forgetting to call a timeout, and was fired the next day.
