#2707. 30 things to be thankful for in the NBA, including Victor Wembanyama's greatness and the Lakers' chemistry

As we approach the 20-game mark in the NBA's regular season, I must say it's been a true joy to watch these teams compete — trying to learn what they're good at, where they struggle and adjusting on the fly. They may not have all the answers quite yet, but the effort is there. It can be easy to sit back and criticize, but ultimately it's more fulfilling to lead with gratitude. Here are 30 things I'm thankful for.

East: Atlanta Hawks • Boston Celtics • Brooklyn Nets • Charlotte Hornets • Chicago Bulls • Cleveland Cavaliers • Detroit Pistons • Indiana Pacers • Miami Heat • Milwaukee Bucks • New York Knicks • Orlando Magic • Philadelphia 76ers • Toronto Raptors • Washington Wizards

West: Dallas Mavericks • Denver Nuggets • Golden State Warriors • Houston Rockets • LA Clippers • Los Angeles Lakers • Memphis Grizzlies • Minnesota Timberwolves • New Orleans Pelicans • Oklahoma City Thunder • Phoenix Suns • Portland Trail Blazers • Sacramento Kings • San Antonio Spurs • Utah Jazz

(Dillon Minshall/Yahoo Sports Illustration)

The month of November. Last year at this time, the Hawks went 10-5 with the ninth-best defensive rating in the league. So far this month they are 8-2 with the fifth-best defensive rating. Having a new level of wing depth thanks to the additions of Aaron Wiggins and Lu Dort has allowed them to balance different lineups through injuries. When the Hawks' defensive activity (see Daniels, Dyson) keeps opponents out of the paint, they get a chance to run and flow.

Their defense. Poor timing (like a recent four-game losing streak) can put a damper on things this time of year, but the Celtics have rarely had to worry about that on the defensive end of the floor. They have consistently defended as a unit, showing early help, swarming on any sort of dribble penetration and working to dictate terms on what types of shots they give up. This will be key as they navigate more and more teams working to trap Jayson Tatum on offense.

Thankfully, this is only the second time in 10 regular seasons that the Nets have started 3-13 (don't ask me how I know). Then again, this is now back-to-back seasons where they have started 3-13, with opponents scoring 129 or more points against them in five of their last seven games. If they can string together some stops, it may feel different. OK, on a brighter note, their broadcast team is elite and the ABA court is fire.

Who doesn't appreciate something that didn't look so fun on paper and then suddenly did? Look at the turnaround from the Bulls, who have found a balance offensively and some effort defensively. Buddy Hield has made 18 3s in his last four games! Nic Claxton is switching (again). Tiago Splitter is unafraid to expand his rotation and stick to what's working out there.

Sometimes reality, an honest conversation and a look in the mirror can be what you need. That's what the Hornets have received as their offense has plummeted and their defense has not held up to the pressure. Kon Knueppel has played only six minutes this season and the Hornets did start 4-12 last year before making a play-in run, so perhaps brighter days are ahead.

Sometimes the leftovers are better than the initial meal. Cleveland's record and consistency are likely not where it thought it would be after last year's deep run. But the moments where you see how much James Harden and Donovan Mitchell impact a game and how the Cavs' bench provides them a different type of energy means there could be a deeper payoff down the line.

Morez Johnson Jr.'s start. Sure, there is a consistency that Cooper Flagg is playing with and an ease that Kyrie Irving displays that sets up shots for everyone to contribute, but let's recognize the energy that Johnson brings to the table and the potential he has on the defensive end of the floor. Weakside communication and rotations should not be ignored, folks.

Their adaptability and understanding how to get the job done. The Nuggets have turned their season around by winning 9 out of 13 games and it feels like they are working to find ways to win different types of games — trusting a level of versatility whether it's an offensive shootout or their defense is getting stops. It also very much helps that Jamal Murray and Nikola Jokić are always at the table.

Their evolution continues to stand out. More movement, more flow, quicker decisions. They will have to find balance and avoid old habits, but continuing to move Ausar Thompson around the board before Jalen Duren returns is a good thing. Cade Cunningham, who sat out Tuesday with knee soreness, has continued to cook no matter who has been in the lineup.

It's Stephen Curry. The Warriors should be thankful they have Stephen Curry — who is averaging 27.8 points per game and shooting 45.7% from 3 at 38 years old — and hope he returns to the court as soon as possible.

The wisdom Fred VanVleet and Marcus Smart have delivered in spades. VanVleet, in particular, just has a way of making the exact play the Rockets need over and over again. He reads defenses to open things up, and makes them pay with shots when they anticipate the pass. Smart's ability to defend multiple positions puts everyone else in a more comfortable spot.

The uncompromising nature in which the Pacers play the game of basketball. No matter who is in the lineup, if shots are falling, if they are getting stops: they are going to work to play Pacers basketball. The ball is going to move, the drives are going to be there, the passes will persist. It might be Andrew Nembhard eating up space, Ivica Zubac making an impact in the paint or Pascal Siakam doing a little bit of everything. They don't stop. Just watch Kelly Oubre.

Brandon Ingram's return. Is there a chance that the talent on the Clippers can finally come together and maximize their ability to be bothersome in the Western Conference? Hope goes a long way, and the more scoring and playmaking the Clippers can have on the court the better. Darius Garland has been game, but the Clippers just need to sustain the good they find rather than reinvent the wheel.

The chemistry that has elevated the Lakers. Luka Dončić has been lights out, but the build around him has continued to pay off. They can get to multiple lineups and get consistent production up and down the roster. You never quite know who could help break a game open for them. Every night you can feel them attempting to accomplish the same goals on each end.

Internal growth. As tremendous as Ty Jerome and Cameron Boozer have been, has anyone been paying attention to the development from Cedric Coward and Cam Spencer? Coward hit 33.8% of his 3s last year and is shooting 44.7% from 3 this year while attempting more of them. Spencer continuously makes plays (and shots) within the flow of what the Grizzlies want to do. They are a team that believes in what they do every night and the internal growth shows it game in and game out.

Davion Mitchell, who has done everything this team has asked him to do and more. The defense speaks for itself, but the playmaking has grown. Mitchell is unafraid to direct the offense, he's reading defenses better, and his paint touches mean more. Mix in some shot-making and he's as happy as Klay Thompson running an empty-side action. Coach Spo has (once again) expanded his menu by mixing in pick-and-rolls or post-ups when needed, too.

Not all charcuterie boards are the same, but you can find the right combinations, even in the wildest ones. Brayden Burries' drives have been fun, but there is a rhythm that is helping him find consistency on the court. Him playing well while Tyler Herro gets all of the buckets is a positive sign. Herro scoring 50 and 32 in back-to-back games while shooting 60%+ from the field is not easy, either.

Consistency. It's not hard to see these Wolves play the game with a level of joy, but they also bring a level of steadiness, particularly on the defensive end. Rudy Gobert patrols the paint, but their ability to use their size and length to switch and bog teams down continues to ignite their transition attack. The fact that they can have this level of lineup consistency on both ends of the floor speaks to a different world in Minnesota.

The growth of Zion Williamson. It's been an up-and-down time in New Orleans, but that shouldn't take away from the Pelicans star's performance. The team has been more willing to switch him defensively and he's worked to hold up. The drives remain, but there's an ease to get to a short pull-up. He's even mixed in 3s on occasion. Tack on the consistent drives from Jeremiah Fears and there is still some potential on this plate.

Every single dish, dunk and drive from Karl-Anthony Towns this season. If last year was about KAT answering questions, this year has been about him delivering problems. The Knicks have been able to lean more into playing through him, opening their offense for everyone. He's also found the gaps to attack, making it tough on bigs and working to punish wings who switch. It also means you really don't know when and where Jalen Brunson is going to attack.

Trusting in your principles in the face of adversity. The Thunder know what they want to work toward and trust they can get there, but each night brings a different test as they navigate injuries. Lineups that used to be a strength (double big/small ball) may present new challenges with this group.

Franz Wagner playmaking. Sometimes you're asked to handle more responsibility or you graduate to a different table this time of year. Since Oct. 30, Wagner has six games where he has 10 or more assists. Prior to that he had done it only two times in his career. It's not just reading defenses, it's bending defenses and understanding what his move can do to force a defense to react. Positive sign for the Magic.

The ability to figure things out and adjust on the fly is always something to be thankful for. It's been a big reason why the Sixers have been able to climb to the top of the Eastern Conference. It may not be the ideal setting, but what in life is? Led by Tyrese Maxey, they are 12-0 in clutch games this season while shooting 50% from the field and 52% from 3 before they have everything figured out. That is a good sign.

Getting back to the basics. Success is not always linear, and just because you do something well once does not mean it will automatically transfer. Returning to the basics of what Phoenix likes to do defensively should help soften some of its offensive issues. It's always good to be self-aware in these moments. It helps that the Suns are 6-2 when Collin Gillespie scores 10 or more points.

Who doesn't love a good homecoming? The Damian Lillard return continues to pay off as the Blazers are able to get the most out of him without putting the total weight of the offense on him. And who doesn't love a good wing? Toumani Camara is a mad man, Sidy Cissoko has continued to defend multiple positions … and they are both making 3s?

It's the ground floor of the Darius Acuff Jr. Experience, and while the wins may not be there right now … sometimes you're in the building for something special before it truly becomes that. Whether it's the straight-line drives, the ability to get to his own shot when he wants or the reps to develop his playmaking, there is a payoff here.

Being able to watch Victor Wembanyama play basketball every single night. There is a moment in almost every game that feels like it's something you've never witnessed before. Criticism is fair, but greatness shouldn't be ignored.

Again, who doesn't appreciate a good wing? Toronto has them in bunches and they all deliver in different ways. The growing comfort of Scottie Barnes when it comes to getting to his shot in the midrange. Collin Murray-Boyles' ability to be everywhere at once on any given possession. Allen Graves and his quick hands; best of luck if you overpenetrate around him.

Opportunities for Darryn Peterson to grow. Injuries have taken their toll on this Jazz roster and the fact that 15 of their 19 games played have been against teams at .500 or above has not helped. But there have been plenty of chances for Peterson to develop as a playmaker and a defender. A lot of the issues feel controllable: the up-and-down ball movement, inconsistent defense and turnovers. You have to hope it gets better, but as Will Hardy said, hope is not a strategy.

Before Monday's loss to the Celtics, the Wizards had not just won three straight games, but had held three straight opponents to sub-100 points scoring. For the season, they have held opponents under 100 five times. They did it three times last year. Effort goes a long way.

#2705. NFL Injury News: 49ers’ Brock Purdy dealing with ankle injury ahead of Seahawks game

As the Seattle Seahawks prepare to welcome Sam Darnold back into the starting lineup, their Week 12 opponents could be missing their MVP favorite quarterback.

San Francisco 49ers star Brock Purdy torched the Minnesota Vikings in a magnificent showing last Sunday in Mexico City, but it did come at somewhat of a cost. #2703. The biggest money-making energy trade looks over. What to buy instead

Is one of the greatest money-making trades of the last few years coming to an end?

No, I'm not talking about Nvidia or any of the data center- or AI-related power companies (although they have been crushed lately too… more on that on CNBC TV. Tune in!).

I'm talking about whether the epic boom in refinery-related stocks is unwinding. Over the last year, the jump in some of these companies has been nothing short of remarkable.

That jump is largely due to massive increases in margins, earnings, and cash flow. Those have occurred because of a big jump in what is called a crack spread. That is the difference between the price of oil and the finished price of refined products such as diesel and jet fuel. For most of this year, spreads have been soaring as disruptions and attacks on facilities and shipping in the Arabian Gulf, Hormuz, and deep inside Russia have played havoc with the market. In the last week or so, however, something big has changed: many of those spreads have started to tighten.

This seismic drop in spreads is causing a selloff in the aforementioned refinery companies.

If spreads continue to fall, these stocks may well keep selling off from recent highs. The upshot for investors may be that, as the shares come down, analysts see more upside. The median price target for Phillips 66 (PSX) now indicates a 20% gain from here, while smaller-cap Par Pacific (PARR) is double that.

Here are a few other key points to focus on heading into the Thanksgiving-shortened week.

I like to keep politics as much at arm's length as possible — we all know that energy and politics (sadly) often go hand in hand. Energy policies coming from Washington, D.C., can drive — or destroy — investment dollars. With all the big shifts in the White House over the last 10 years (four years of Trump, four years of Biden, and now 2 years into Trump #2), energy policies can blow through Washington faster than a summer squall hits a wind turbine. But because federal policies can mean so much for specific companies, sectors, and investments, it's important to follow the news flow.

That said, I'd like to take a moment to introduce you to the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act, known as BAAJA (and pronounced like the state in Mexico). Like much legislation, BAAJA is an obscure but important bill working its way through the American Congress. BAAJA does a lot of things, but what Power Insider readers care about is its focus on energy permitting reform. In a nutshell, BAAJA would help speed up the building of new electricity infrastructure. Again, that's an overly simplistic view of a big bill, but it needs to be on your radar. The utility trade group Edison Electric Institute came out in favor of the legislation this month after the industry received some 11th-hour changes. See more about it in our recent interview with EEI President Drew Maloney here.

The bill passed the U.S. Senate in a vote late Thursday night. Now it moves to the House. Support for its final passage is also coming from the big LNG trade group, whose executive director said he "applauds the Senate for its bipartisan effort to pass meaningful, bipartisan, and durable permitting reforms aimed at delivering greater predictability, consistency, and certainty in the permitting, production, and export process for U.S. LNG. This legislation moves us closer to untangling red tape and eliminating overlapping agency reviews that can keep U.S. energy projects delayed for years."

The Natural Gas Supply Association also chimed in, saying it urges Congress to see the bill as "a resource-agnostic and balanced reform agenda for boosting American energy security. It keeps strong environmental reviews in place, but it draws a firm line against the endless procedural delays that tie critical energy projects in knots for a decade."

In my view, passing BAAJA is an important test of Congress's appetite for helping the energy and electrical power industry, lowering costs, and speeding time to market. The old adage was correct: time is money. Literally. If BAAJA moves on through the House, we could see an upside move in some utility stocks and any company related to energy infrastructure.

Speaking of politics, the news is getting lost in the massive Anthropic (ANT) IPO, but the Texas Commission on Environmental Quality (TCEQ) — whose importance I wrote about in the last edition — just decided to resume permitting for data centers. The Texas Tribune did a nice write-up on it, and you can read it here.

It's tough to say, but it's true. The two are inextricably linked by policies created at both the federal and state levels. As I wrote in the last edition, some federally elected officials may have less impact on state-level issues — like the data center buildout — but they certainly have power over national laws. While many of you are enthralled, disgusted, or confused by sordid tales of Nvidia and Jensen-gate, keep one eye open on what happens in Washington, D.C.

Have a great week, and have a wonderful Thanksgiving, everyone.

Perhaps the big investor story — which is, like most things lately, related to Jensen-gate and Nvidia—is the sizable selloff in many AI, neocloud, and data center power-related stocks.

Painful for current shareholders, but big drops can also bring big opportunities. Wall Street believes that's the case, at least right now. Look at some of the potential upside based on the stocks' prices versus their price targets.

We spoke with the CEO of Applied Digital (APLD) about all of this on CNBC last week, and you can watch it here. He highlighted how the market may be getting these stock stories wrong because, at least with his company, he has long-term contracts with solid-credit companies. In that way, Applied Digital is more like a landlord than a "pure-play" AI company.

My take → While I'm not going to recommend a specific stock, I would add that it is critical for investors to understand the differences between these companies. While they may have some things in common, they are also different, with different business models, customers, leases, and sources of revenue.

Speaking of recommendations… here are two ideas for you:

A recent decline presents a nice opportunity in SOLV Energy (MWH). UBS analyst Jon Windham upgrades SOLV to buy from neutral, believing the market is "underappreciating several key tailwinds for SOLV given uncertainty around data center demand and higher interest rates." UBS highlights how the stock has fallen lately despite making two deals and beating and raising recent guidance. UBS says now is an "attractive entry point" for SOLV. (Side note: MWH is a great stock ticker.)

While markets overall fall, BMO remains bullish on Bloom Energy (BE). After speaking with the company, the analyst is upgrading Bloom to outperform, saying he remains "increasingly confident of BE's commercial traction" and that he still has conviction even with the recent drop in shares. While analyst Ameet Thakkar did cut his target to $230 from $260, that's still 50% upside from the current price. Bloom shares have tumbled 45% since October 1.

I was recently asked for my opinion on the price of oil. Here's my take. The market clearly wants to push oil contract prices lower and has for a few months. But, as I've said and reported for a while, don't confuse the futures (paper) price with the actual price. Yes, shipping rates and insurance rates have come down from recent highs — and there is a good amount of oil now flowing through Hormuz — but the cost of a physical barrel of crude is still well above the futures contract. If we get real, longer-lasting peace around Iran, oil prices will fall. There is plenty of oil in the world. That said, if bombs and missiles start firing again, oil prices will firm. I know that's a non-answer answer, but the oil market is truly headline-driven right now. My late good friend Boone Pickens (later in life, he didn't like being called by the "T" in his first name) used to avoid giving a set price target and instead give a directional quote, so I'll do the same: the odds of oil being lower in two months than it is today are 75%.

Last week, I had a one-on-one with Vimal Kapur, chair and CEO of Honeywell International (HON), at an RBC conference in New York City. While the conversation wasn't filmed, I can tell you that Kapur offered investors reassurance that his order book is doing very, very well. This is important because Honeywell is huge in the things that make other things work. Its sensors go into a ton of energy facilities, from refineries to LNG facilities. Honeywell is a leading indicator of spending and buildouts, so if it keeps getting orders, it's a good sign for macro energy.

Bottom line: Watch Honeywell stock as a potential "tell."

This week's Inside Line interview takes a different approach. We hit our friend Craig Johnson up for actionable insights into energy and stocks. Craig is managing director and chief market technician at Piper Sandler. He recently appeared on Power Lunch to talk about markets and stocks. He mentioned being bullish on energy, so we wanted to dig in just a little bit more.

This week, we had the pleasure of chatting with Balaji Krishnamurthy, president of Chevron Australia. We spoke at length about natural gas, the impact of disruptions, and long-term demand from Chevron customers in the Asia-Pacific region.

This is a bit of a local RBI for the New York City area, but one I felt was important to highlight. New York's electric grid operator just came out with a massive warning for everyone in our region: in just a couple of years, there may not be enough electricity supply to power our needs. A new report shows a deficit in seven years, with a nearly 2 GW power hole in a decade. That's a number of big power plants' worth of energy. Get your solar panels on and buy that backup generator! I'll call this one random, but scary.

Catch up with more on energy including interviews and video content from CNBC and Power Insider.

#2701. The Extra Reward for Owning Stocks Over Bonds Has Disappeared

#2700. Nets vs. Bucks preview: Brooklyn looks to end five-game losing streak

MILWAUKEE, WISCONSIN – NOVEMBER 15: Tyler Herro #11 of the Milwaukee Bucks runs down court during the first half of the game against the Atlanta Hawks at Fiserv Forum on November 15, 2026 in Milwaukee, Wisconsin. NOTE TO USER: User expressly acknowledges and agrees that, by downloading and or using this photograph, User is consenting to the terms and conditions of the Getty Images License Agreement. (Photo by John Fisher/Getty Images) | Getty Images

Bad basketball teams find ways to lose games. On Monday night, the Brooklyn Nets tied the game against the Minnesota Timberwolves at five early in the first quarter. And that was the closest they got. Brooklyn never led and trailed by as much as 30 points on national TV. They lost by 26 points, 142-116, withe the losing streak sitting at five entering Wednesday's matchup against the 2-16 Milwaukee Bucks.

They've had their fair share of struggles this year.

The Bucks are at the bottom of the NBA standings, but they had a good time in Canada on Monday night. They took on the Toronto Raptors and won 116-111. The win snapped their 13-game losing streak, which was the second-longest in franchise history. You take the wins where you can get them.

Matchup: Brooklyn Nets (3-13) vs. Milwaukee Bucks (2-16)

By the numbers, these are the two worst defensive teams in the NBA. Brooklyn allows 120.3 points per 100 possessions, worst in the NBA. Milwaukee isn't that far behind at 119.8 points per 100 possessions — the second-worst in the NBA. These two teams allow easy buckets and bad defense makes games a lot harder to sit through if you're rooting for the team with the bad defense.

Look for this one to be a high scoring affair and whoever can put together enough stops on defense should walk away with the win.

Where the Nets have doomed themselves this year is in the turnover department. Brooklyn commits the second most turnovers in the league this year and when you combine that with a bad defense, it explains why this team is in the mud.

If the Nets want to start putting wins together, they're going to need real play out of Michael Porter Jr. MPJ tied a season low in field goal attempts with seven on Monday night and was virtually non-existent out there. It's not easy when the team is in a rut, but as one of the vets on the team and a former NBA champion, more is expected of MPJ. He's admittedly trying to find his rhythm, and tonight would be a good time to find the right mix heading into a weekend back-to-back.

Myles Turner is another one of those players you forget has been in the league a long time. This is his 12th season in the league and he figures to be a trade candidate this winter. In theory, Turner provides floor spacing, rim protection, and deep postseason experience for any teams out there in the market. The Raptors are certainly looking for help at the five and if they ever get fully healthy, maybe Turner is the player that pushes them over the top.

Herro was the centerpiece of the trade that sent Giannis Antetokounmpo to the Miami Heat, and Bucks management is looking to see how he fits into their future plans. From Dalton Sell at Behind the Bucks Pass:

"With each passing game, it's becoming clearer that Brayden Burries and Kasparas Jakucionis need as much playing time as possible. Just 21 and 20 years old, they are oozing the type of potential that could make them the franchise's backcourt for the next decade plus. It's hard to let them entirely off the leash when players like Herro are commanding so many minutes, though.

If the Milwaukee Bucks truly believe in these two, it would make sense to move Herro and potentially even other guards like Ryan Rollins to unleash the duo. It wouldn't hurt to collect assets as well. The Bucks should be on the hunt for future draft picks and young players, and if someone offers a solid combination of those things, it would make trading Tyler Herro that much easier to stomach."

We'll see. Herro has been on a hot streak of late as he's shot above 50% from the field in three consecutive games.

Mikel Brown Jr got his first start of the season, and he'll get all the minutes he can handle now that Nolan Traore is out for the next few months. Brown Jr. looked good in his time out there against the Wolves with 17 points, and we'll see if the Nets let him play 30 minutes amid an early-season minutes restriction. Brown Jr's development is the only thing that is preventing Nets fans online from staging a full-on revolt at this point.

Even as the losses pile up for Brooklyn, Brown Jr playing well would at least give a sense of hope that things are heading in the right direction. The next step is leading the Nets in a win they should get.

Grand Theft Auto VI has taken over the world and the game looks absolutely sensational. With that in mind, let's revisit some of my favorite missions from a previous era of GTA.

#2699. Steelers release Aaron Rodgers after blasting Mike McCarthy

It's all but official: The Pittsburgh Steelers are releasing Aaron Rodgers in what's been an insane 24 hours for fans of the black and gold brand of football.

On Wednesday, news broke that the Steelers were releasing 42-year-old quarterback Rodgers hours after he made his insane allegations on The Pat McAfee Show, which included accusing head coach Mike McCarthy of missing walkthroughs and falling asleep during team meetings.

"SOURCES: Steelers are expected to release quarterback Aaron Rodgers, a decision that was hastened when Rodgers went on Pat McAfee and accused Mike McCarthy of falling asleep in meetings and saying the whole team knows something is physically wrong with the 63-year-old coach," Gerry Dulac of Pittsburgh Post-Gazette reported.

Speaking earlier on The Pat McAfee Show, Rodgers revealed that although he was concerned about McCarthy's overall physical health, he presented an ultimatum: either change the offensive play-calling or release him if those changes weren't made or he was benched. Rodgers also made it clear to listeners that he would not return to Pittsburgh after the tumultuous ending.

Ultimately, if Rodgers' goal in making these fiery claims about McCarthy and the Steelers organization was to be released, he certainly got what he was looking for. Here's hoping, though, that Rodgers doesn't wind up playing for an AFC North rival or a potential playoff contender that could make Pittsburgh's road to the playoffs a little more difficult.
